Hop-Constrained Metric Embeddings and their Applications Thanks: An extended abstract of this paper appeared in the proceedings of FOCS 21 [ Fil21 ] . This short preliminary includes only the introduction, and statements of the results.
Arnold Filtser Bar-Ilan University Email: arnold273@gmail.com

Abstract
In network design problems, such as compact routing, the goal is to route packets between nodes using the (approximated) shortest paths. A desirable property of these routes is a small number of hops, which makes them more reliable, and reduces the transmission costs. Following the overwhelming success of stochastic tree embeddings for algorithmic design, Haeupler, Hershkowitz, and Zuzic (STOC’21) studied hop-constrained Ramsey-type metric embeddings into trees. Specifically, embedding f : G ⁡ ( V , E ) → T f:G(V,E)\rightarrow T has Ramsey hop-distortion ( t , M , β , h ) (t,M,\beta,h) (here t , β , h ≥ 1 t,\beta,h\geq 1 and M ⊆ V M\subseteq V ) if ∀ u ∈ M , v ∈ V \forall u\in M,v\in V , d G ( β ⋅ h ) ​ ( u , v ) ≤ d T ​ ( u , v ) ≤ t ⋅ d G ( h ) ​ ( u , v ) d_{G}^{(\beta\cdot h)}(u,v)\leq d_{T}(u,v)\leq t\cdot d_{G}^{(h)}(u,v) . t t is called the distortion, β \beta is called the hop-stretch, and d G ( h ) ​ ( u , v ) d_{G}^{(h)}(u,v) denotes the minimum weight of a u − v u-v path with at most h h hops. Haeupler et al. constructed embedding where M M contains 1 − ϵ 1-\epsilon fraction of the vertices and β = t = O ⁡ ( log 2 ⁡ n ϵ ) \beta=t=O(\frac{\log^{2}n}{\epsilon}) . They used their embedding to obtain multiple bicriteria approximation algorithms for hop-constrained network design problems. In this paper, we first improve the Ramsey-type embedding to obtain parameters t = β = O ~ ​ ( log ⁡ n ) ϵ t=\beta=\frac{\tilde{O}(\log n)}{\epsilon} , and generalize it to arbitrary distortion parameter t t (in the cost of reducing the size of M M ). This embedding immediately implies polynomial improvements for all the approximation algorithms from Haeupler et al. . Further, we construct hop-constrained clan embeddings (where each vertex has multiple copies), and use them to construct bicriteria approximation algorithms for the group Steiner tree problem, matching the state of the art of the non-constrained version. Finally, we use our embedding results to construct hop-constrained distance oracles, distance labeling, and most prominently, the first hop-constrained compact routing scheme with provable guarantees. All our metric data structures almost match the state of the art parameters of the non hop-constrained versions.



1
Introduction

Low distortion metric embeddings provide a powerful algorithmic toolkit, with applications ranging from approximation/sublinear/online/distributed algorithms
[
LLR95
,
AMS99
,
BCL+18
,
KKM+12
]
to machine learning
[
GKK17
]
, biology
[
HBK+03
]
, and vision
[
AS03
]
.
A basic approach for solving a problem in a “hard” metric space
(X,d_{X})
, is to embed the points
X
into a “simple” metric space
(Y,d_{Y})
while preserving all pairwise distances up to small multiplicative factor.
Then one solve the problem in
Y
, and “pull-back” the solution into
X
.

A highly desirable target space is trees, as many hard problems become easy once the host space is a tree metric. Fakcharoenphol, Rao, and Talwar
[
FRT04
]
(improving over
[
AKPW95
,
Bar96
,
Bar98
]
, see also
[
Bar04
]
) showed that every
n
point metric space could be embedded into distribution
\mathcal{D}
over dominating trees with expected distortion
O(\log n)
. Formally,
\forall x,y\in X
,
T\in\mathrm{supp}(\mathcal{D})
,
d_{X}(x,y)\leq d_{T}(x,y)
, and
\mathbb{E}_{T\sim\mathcal{D}}[d_{T}(x,y)]\leq O(\log n)\cdot d_{X}(x,y)
.
This stochastic embedding enjoyed tremendous success and has numerous applications. However, the distortion guarantee is only in expectation.
A different solution is Ramsey type embeddings which have a worst case guarantee, however only w.r.t. a subset of the points. Specifically, Mendel and Naor
[
MN07
]
(see also
[
BFM86
,
BBM06
,
BLMN05a
,
NT12
,
BGS16
,
ACE+20
,
Bar21
]
) showed that for every parameter
k
, every
n
-point metric space contains a subset
M
of at least
n^{1-\frac{1}{k}}
points, where
X
could be embedded into a tree such that all the distances in
M\times X
are preserved up to an
O(k)
multiplicative factor. Formally,
\forall x,y\in X
,
d_{X}(x,y)\leq d_{T}(x,y)
, and
\forall x\in M,y\in X
,
d_{T}(x,y)\leq O(k)\cdot d_{X}(x,y)
.
Finally, in order to obtain worst case guarantee w.r.t. all point pairs, the author and Hung
[
FL21
]
introduced clan embeddings into trees. Here each point
x
is mapped into a subset
f(x)\subseteq Y
which is referred to as the copies of
x
, with a special chief copy
\chi(x)\in f(x)
. For every parameter
k
,
[
FL21
]
constructed distribution over dominating clan embeddings such that for every pair of vertices
x,y
, some copy of
x
is close to the chief of
y
:
d_{X}(x,y)\leq\min_{x^{\prime}\in f(x),y^{\prime}\in f(y)}d_{T}(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})\leq\min_{x^{\prime}\in f(x)}d_{T}(x^{\prime},\chi(y))\leq O(k)\cdot d_{X}(x,y)
, and the expected number of copies of every vertex is bounded:
\mathbb{E}[|f(x)|]=O(n^{\frac{1}{k}})
.

In many applications, the metric space is the shortest path metric
d_{G}
of a weighted graph
G=(V,E,w)
.
In some scenarios, in addition to metric distances, there are hop-constrains. For instance, one may wish to route a packet between two nodes, using a path with at most
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}
hops,
1
1

1

To facilitate the reading of the paper, all numbers referring to hops are colored in red.
i.e. the number of edges in the path.
Such hop constrains are desirable as each transmission causes delays, which are non-negligible when the number of transmissions is large
[
AT11
,
BF18
]
.
Another advantage is that low-hop routes are more reliable: if each transmission is prone to failure with a certain probability, then low-hop routes are much more likely to reach their destination
[
BF18
,
WA88
,
RAJ12
]
.
Electricity and telecommunications distribution network configuration problems include hop constraints that limit the maximum number of edges between a customer and its feeder
[
BF18
]
, and there are many other (practical) network design problems with hop constraints
[
LCM99
,
BA92
,
GPdSV03
,
GM03
,
PS03
]
.
Hop-constrained network approximation is often used in parallel computing
[
Coh00
,
ASZ20
]
, as the number of hops governs the number of required parallel rounds (e.g. in Dijkstra).
Finally, there is an extensive work on approximation algorithms for connectivity problems like spanning tree and Steiner tree with hop constraints
[
Rav94
,
KP97
,
MRS+98
,
AFH+05
,
KLS05
,
KP09
,
HKS09
,
KS16
]
, and for some generalizations in
[
HHZ21b
]
.

Given a weighted graph
G=(V,E,w)
,
d_{G}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(h)}}(u,v)
denotes the minimum weight of a
u
-
v
path containing at most
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}
hops.
Following the tremendous success of tree embeddings, Haeupler, Hershkowitz and Zuzic
[
HHZ21b
]
suggested to study hop-constrained tree embeddings. That is, embedding the vertex set
V
into a tree
T
, such that
d_{T}(u,v)
will approximate the
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}
-hop-constrained distance
d_{G}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(h)}}(u,v)
.
Unfortunately,
[
HHZ21b
]
showed that
d_{G}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(h)}}
is very far from being a metric space, and the distortion of every embedding of
d_{G}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(h)}}
is unbounded.
To overcome this issue,
[
HHZ21b
]
allowed a slack not only in the distance approximation (distortion) but also in the allowed number of hops (hop-stretch). Specifically, allowing bi-criteria approximation in the following sense:
d_{G}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(\beta\cdot h)}}(u,v)\leq d_{T}(u,v)\leq t\cdot d^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(h)}}_{G}(u,v)
, for some parameters
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\beta}
, and
t
.
However, even this relaxation is not enough as long as
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\beta\cdot h}<n-1
.
To see this, consider the unweighted
n
-path
P_{n}=\{v_{0},\dots,v_{n-1}\}
. Then
d_{G}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(n-2)}}(v_{0},v_{n-1})=\infty
, implying that for every metric
X
over
V
,
\max_{i}\{d_{X}(v_{i},v_{i+1})\}\geq\frac{1}{n-1}\sum_{i}d_{X}(v_{i},v_{i+1})\geq\frac{d_{X}(v_{0},v_{n-1})}{n-1}\geq\frac{d_{G}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(n-2)}}(v_{0},v_{n-1})}{n-1}=\infty
.
As a result, some pairs have distortion
\infty
, and hence no bi-criteria metric embedding is possible. In particular, there is no hop-constrained counterpart for
[
FRT04
]
.

To overcome this barrier,
[
HHZ21b
]
studied hop-constrained Ramsey-type tree embeddings.
2
2

2

[
HHZ21b
]
called this embedding “partial metric distribution”, rather than Ramsey-type.
Their main result, is that every
n
-point weighted graph
G=(V,E,w)
with polynomial aspect ratio,
3
3

3

The
aspect ratio
(sometimes referred to as spread), is the ratio between the maximal to minimal weight in
G

\frac{\max_{e\in E}w(e)}{\min_{e\in E}w(e)}
. Often in the literature the aspect ratio is defined as
\frac{\max_{u,v\in V}d_{G}(u,v)}{\min_{u\neq v\in V}d_{G}(u,v)}
:
the ratio between the maximal to minimal distance in
G
, which is equivalent up to a factor
n
to the definition used here.

and parameter
\epsilon\in(0,1)
, there is a distribution over pairs
(T,M)
, where
M\subseteq V
, and
T
is a tree with
M
as its vertex set such that

\forall u,v\in M,\qquad d_{G}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(O(\frac{\log^{2}n}{\epsilon})\cdot h)}}(u,v)\leq d_{T}(u,v)\leq O(\frac{\log^{2}n}{\epsilon})\cdot d_{G}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(h)}}(u,v)\penalty\ ,

and for every
v\in V
,
\Pr[v\in M]\geq 1-\epsilon
(referred to as inclusion probability). Improving the distortion, and hop-stretch, was left as the “main open question” in
[
HHZ21b
]
.

Question 1

(
[
HHZ21b
]
)
.

Is it possible to construct better hop-constrained Ramsey-type tree embeddings? What are the optimal parameters?

Adding hop-constrains to a problem often makes it considerably harder. For example, obtaining
o(\log n)
approximation for the hop-constrained MST problem (where the hop-diameter must be bounded by
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}
) is NP-hard
[
BKP01
]
, while MST is simple problem without it. Similarly, for every
{\epsilon>0}
, obtaining
o(2^{\log^{1-\epsilon}}n)
-approximation for the hop-constrained Steiner forest problem is NP-hard
[
DKR16
]
(while constant approximation is possible without hop constrains
[
AKR95
]
).

[
HHZ21b
]
used their hop-constrained Ramsey-type tree embeddings to construct many approximation algorithms.
Roughly, given that some problem has (non constrained) approximation ratio
\alpha
for trees,
[
HHZ21b
]
obtain a bicriteria approximation for the hop-constrained problem with cost
O(\alpha\cdot\log^{3}n)
, and hop-stretch
O(\log^{3}n)
.
For example, in the group Steiner tree problem we are given
k
sets
g_{1},\dots,g_{k}\subseteq V
, and a root vertex
r\in V
. The goal is to construct a minimal weight tree spanning the vertex
r
and at least one vertex from each group (set)
g_{i}
. In the hop-constrained version of the problem, we are given in addition a hop-bound
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}
, and the requirement is to find a minimum weight subgraph
H
, such that for every
g_{i}
,
\mathrm{hop}_{H}(r,g_{i})\leq{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}
.
Denote by
\mathrm{OPT}
the weight of the optimal solution.
In the non-constrained version, where the input metric is a tree,
[
GKR00
]
provided a
O(\log n\cdot\log k)
approximation. Using the scheme of
[
HHZ21b
]
, one can find a solution
H
of weight
O(\log^{4}n\cdot\log k)\cdot\mathrm{OPT}
, such that for every
g_{i}
,
\mathrm{hop}_{H}(r,g_{i})\leq{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}O(\log^{3}n)\cdot h}
.
The best approximation for the (non-constrained) group Steiner tree problem is
O(\log^{2}n\cdot\log k)

[
GKR00
]
.
Clearly, every improvement over the hop-constrained Ramsey-type embedding will imply better bicriteria approximation algorithms. However, even if one would use
[
HHZ21b
]
framework on optimal Ramsey-type embeddings (ignoring hop-constrains), the cost factors will be inferior compared to the non-constrained versions (specifically
O(\log^{3}n\cdot\log k)
for GST).
It is interesting to understand whether there is a separation between the cost of a bicriteria approximation for hop-constrained problems to their non-constrained counterparts. A more specific question is the following:

Question 2
.

Could one match the state of the art cost approximation of the (non hop-constrained) group Steiner tree problem while having
\mathrm{polylog}(n)
hop-stretch?

Metric data structures such as distance oracles, distance labeling, and compact routing schemes are extensively studied, and widely used throughout algorithmic design.
A natural question is whether one can construct similar data structures w.r.t. hop-constrained distances.
Most prominently is the question of compact routing scheme
[
TZ01
]
, where one assigns each node in a network a short label and small local routing table, such that packets could be routed throughout the network with routes not much longer than the shortest paths.
Hop-constrained routing is a natural question due to it’s clear advantages (bounding transmission delays, and increasing reliability). Indeed, numerous papers developed different heuristics for different models of hop-constrained routing.
4
4

4

In fact, at the time of writing, the search ”hop-constrained” + routing in google scholar outputs 1,020 papers.

However, to the best knowledge of the author, no prior provable guarantees were provided for hop-constrained compact routing scheme.

Question 3
.

What hop-constrained compact routing schemes are possible?

1.1
Our contribution

1.1.1
Metric embeddings

Figure 1:
An illustration of an embedding of the unweighted
3\times 6
grid into an ultrametric with Ramsey hop-distortion
(t=3,M=\{a,b,c,p,q,r\},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\beta=4},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h=1})
. In the ultrametric, the distance between a pair of vertices equals to the label of the least common ancestor.
The ultrametric defines an hierarchical partition, which corresponds to the blue and green dashed lines on the left.
The embedding has hop stretch
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\beta=4}
as
\forall u,v\in V
,
d_{G}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(4\cdot 1)}}(u,v)\leq d_{U}(u,v)
. Note that
4
is tight, as
d_{G}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(4)}}(a,i)=4=d_{U}(a,i)<d_{G}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(3)}}(a,i)=\infty
. The embedding has distortion
t=3
as
\forall u\in M,v\in V
,
d_{U}(u,v)\leq t\cdot d_{G}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(1)}}(u,v)
. Note that
3
is tight, as
d_{U}(a,b)=3=3\cdot d_{G}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(1)}}(a,b)
.
Interestingly, the exact same embedding also has Ramsey hop-distortion
(t=4,M=\{a,b,c,d,e,f,m,n,o,p,q,r\},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\beta=4},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h=1})
.

Here we present our metric embedding results. Our embeddings will be into ultrametrics (a.k.a. HST), which are structured type of trees having the strong triangle inequality (see
Definition

1
). We denote ultrametrics usually with
U
.
Our first contribution is an improved Ramsey-type hop-constrained embedding into trees, partially solving
1
.
Embedding
f:V(G)\rightarrow U
is said to have Ramsey hop-distortion
(t,M,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\beta},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h})
(here
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\beta}>1
) if
\forall u,v\in V
,
d_{G}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(\beta\cdot h)}}(u,v)\leq d_{U}(u,v)
, and
\forall u\in M,v\in V
,
d_{U}(u,v)\leq t\cdot d_{G}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(h)}}(u,v)
. See
Definition

4
for a formal definition and
Figure

1
for an illustration.
In
Theorem

1
bellow we construct a distribution over Ramsey-type embeddings with inclusion probability
\Omega(n^{-\frac{1}{k}})
(that is every vertex
v
belongs to the set
M
with probability
\Omega(n^{-\frac{1}{k}})
), and every embedding in the support has Ramsey hop-distortion
(O(k),M,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}O(k\cdot\log n)},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h})
. That is the distance between every pair
u,v
(where at least one of them belong to
M
) has distortion
O(k)
and hop stretch
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}O(k\cdot\log n)}
. We also allow taking the inclusion probability to the extreme, where each vertex belongs to
M
with probability
1-\epsilon
. Then the distortion is
O(\frac{\log n}{\epsilon})
, while the hop-stretch is
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}O(\frac{\log^{2}n}{\epsilon})}
.

Theorem 1

(Hop-Constrained Ramsey Embedding)
.

Consider an
n
-vertex graph
G=(V,E,w)
with polynomial aspect ratio, and parameters
k,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}\in[n]
.
Then there is a distribution
\mathcal{D}
over dominating ultrametrics,
such that:

1.

Every
U\in\mathrm{supp}(\mathcal{D})
, has Ramsey hop-distortion
(O(k),M,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}O(k\cdot\log n)},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h})
, where
M\subseteq V
is a random variable.

2.

For every
v\in V
,
\Pr_{U\sim\mathcal{D}}[v\in M]\geq\Omega(n^{-\frac{1}{k}})
.

In addition, for every
\epsilon\in(0,1)
, there is distribution
\mathcal{D}
as above such that every
U\in\mathrm{supp}(\mathcal{D})
has Ramsey hop-distortion
(O(\frac{\log n}{\epsilon}),M,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}O(\frac{\log^{2}n}{\epsilon})},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h})
, and for every
v\in V
,
\Pr[v\in M]\geq 1-\epsilon
.

Theorem

1
is optimized for the distortion parameter (
O(k)/O(\frac{\log n}{\epsilon})
).
Indeed, ignoring the hop-distortion (e.g. setting
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h=n}
), the tradeoff in
Theorem

1
between the distortion to the inclusion probability is asymptotically tight (see
[
BBM06
,
FL21
]
).
However, it is yet unclear what is the best possible hop-stretch obtainable with asymptotically optimal distortion-inclusion probability trade-off.
Interestingly, in
Theorem

2
we show that by increasing the distortion by a
\log\log n
factor, we can obtain a sub-logarithmic hop-stretch. Further, in this version we can also drop the assumption that the aspect ratio is polynomial.

Theorem 2

(Hop-Constrained Ramsey Embedding with small hop-stretch and arbitrary aspect-ratio)
.

Consider an
n
-vertex graph
G=(V,E,w)
, and parameters
k,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}\in[n]
.
Then there is a distribution
\mathcal{D}
over dominating ultrametrics with
V
as leafs, such that every
U\in\mathrm{supp}(\mathcal{D})
has Ramsey hop-distortion
(O(k\cdot\log\log n),M,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}O(k\cdot\log\log n)},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h})
, where
M\subseteq V
is a random variable, and
\forall v\in V
,
\Pr_{U\sim\mathcal{D}}[v\in M]\geq\Omega(n^{-\frac{1}{k}})

In addition, for every
\epsilon\in(0,1)
, there is distribution
\mathcal{D}
as above such that every
U\in\mathrm{supp}(\mathcal{D})
has Ramsey hop-distortion
(O(\frac{\log n\cdot\log\log n}{\epsilon}),M,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}O(\frac{\log n\cdot\log\log n}{\epsilon})},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h})
, and
\forall v\in V
,
\Pr[v\in M]\geq 1-\epsilon
.

Compering to
[
HHZ21b
]
,
[
HHZ21b
]
provides results for inclusion probability in the
1-\epsilon
parameter regime, while our
Theorems

1
and
2
hold in a much wider spectrum of parameters.
Fixing inclusion probability
1-\epsilon
, in
Theorem

1
we improved the distortion by a quadratic factor in the dependence on
n
, while keeping the hop-stretch in-tact.
In
Theorem

2
we improve both distortion and hop-stretch by almost a quadratic factor (in the dependence on
n
).

Figure 2:
An illustration of a clan embedding of the unweighted
3\times 6
grid into an ultrametric with hop distortion
(t=4,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\beta=5},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h=1})
.
The copies of each vertex
v
are denoted by a subset of
v_{1},v_{2},v_{3}
. The vertex representing the copy chosen to be the chief has a red underline.
The clan embedding has hop stretch
\beta=5
, indeed
\forall u,v\in V,\penalty\ \penalty\ d^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(5\cdot 1)}}_{G}(x,y)\leq\min_{u^{\prime}\in f(u),v^{\prime}\in f(v)}d_{U}(u^{\prime},v^{\prime})
.
Note that
5
is tight, as
d^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(5)}}_{G}(a,l)=5=d_{U}(a_{1},l_{1})<d^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(4)}}_{G}(a,l)=\infty
.
The clan embedding has distortion
t=4
, indeed
\forall u,v\in V,\penalty\ \penalty\ \min_{u^{\prime}\in f(u)}d_{U}(u^{\prime},\chi(v))\leq 4\cdot d^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(1)}}_{G}(u,v)
. Note that
4
is tight, as

\min_{j^{\prime}\in f(j)}d_{U}(j^{\prime},\chi(g))=d_{U}(j_{1},g_{2})=4=4\cdot d^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(1)}}_{G}(j,g)
.

The clan embedding has hop-path-distortion
(4,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h=2})
. Consider the
2
-respecting path
(e,h,k)
in
G
of weight
2
. Then the copies
e_{2},h_{2},k_{1}
in
U
corespond to a “path” of weight
8
. From the other hand the path
(e,h,k,n)
is not
2
-respecting, and indeed there is no corresponding path in
U
of finite weight.

Next, we construct hop-constrained clan embedding
(f,\chi)
. Recall that clan
embedding is a one to many embedding
f:V(G)\rightarrow 2^{U}
(where the vertices in
f(v)
called copies of
v
), and a choice of chiefs
\chi:V(G)\rightarrow U
(where
\forall v,\penalty\ \chi(v)\in f(v)
).
Clan embedding
(f,\chi)
is said to have hop-distortion
(t,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\beta},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h})
if it is dominating, that is distance in
U
between every two copies is at least the
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\beta\cdot h}
hop-distance, and the distance from some copy to the chief is distorted by at most a
t
factor (w.r.t. to the
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}
hop distance). Formally:

\forall u,v\in V,\penalty\ \penalty\ d^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(\beta\cdot h)}}_{G}(x,y)\leq\min_{u^{\prime}\in f(u),v^{\prime}\in f(v)}d_{U}(u^{\prime},v^{\prime})\leq\min_{u^{\prime}\in f(u)}d_{U}(u^{\prime},\chi(v))\leq t\cdot d^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(h)}}_{G}(u,v)\penalty\ .

See
Definition

3
for a formal definition, and
Figure

2
for an illustration.
When constructing a clan embedding the goal is to minimize the trade-off between the hop-distortion, to the number of copies each vertex has.
In
Theorem

3
, assuming polynomial aspect ratio, for

k\geq 1
we construct a distribution of clan embeddings with hop distortion
\left(O(k),{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}O(k\cdot\log n)},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}\right)
, while every vertex has only
O(n^{\frac{1}{k}})
copies in expectation.
Alternatively, it is also possible to get only
1+\epsilon
copies in expectation (that is, most vertices will have a single copy), while all the clan embeddings in the support will have hop-distortion
\left(O(\frac{\log n}{\epsilon}),{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}O(\frac{\log^{2}n}{\epsilon})},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}\right)
.

In addition to the hop-distortion guarantee, in
Theorems

3
and
4
we also provide hop-path-distortion guarantee. Roughly speaking,
a path
P=(v_{0},v_{1},\dots,v_{m})
is
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}
-respecting if the induced graph
G[P]
(note that
P
might not be simple) does not contain short paths with more than
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}
-hops (see
Definition

6
).
A one-to-many embedding
f
has hop-path-distortion
(t,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h})
if for every
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}
-respecting path
P=(v_{0},v_{1},\dots,v_{m})
, one can choose copies
v_{i}^{\prime}\in f(v_{i})
such that the path
P^{\prime}=(v_{0}^{\prime},v_{1}^{\prime},\dots,v_{m}^{\prime})
in
U
has total weight at most
t\cdot w_{G}(P)
.
Path-distortion was originally introduced by Bartal and Mendel
[
BM04
]
in the context of multi-embeddings of general graphs (without
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}
-respecting requirement), and was shown to be useful for approximation algorithms (e.g. group Steiner tree).
See
Definition

7
for a formal definition.

Theorem 3

(Clan embedding into ultrametric)
.

Consider an
n
-vertex graph
G=(V,E,w)
with polynomial aspect ratio, and parameters
k,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}\in[n]
.
Then there is a distribution
\mathcal{D}
over clan embeddings
(f,\chi)
into ultrametrics with hop-distortion
\left(O(k),{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}O(k\cdot\log n)},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}\right)
, hop-path-distortion
\left({O(k\cdot\log n)},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}\right)
, and such that for every vertex
v\in V
,
\mathbb{E}_{f\sim\mathcal{D}}[|f(v)|]\leq O(n^{\frac{1}{k}})
.

In addition, for every
\epsilon\in(0,1)
, there is distribution
\mathcal{D}
as above such that every
U\in\mathrm{supp}(\mathcal{D})
has hop-distortion
\left(O(\frac{\log n}{\epsilon}),{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}O(\frac{\log^{2}n}{\epsilon})},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}\right)
, hop-path-distortion
\left({O(\frac{\log^{2}n}{\epsilon})},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}\right)
, and such that for every vertex
v\in V
,
\mathbb{E}_{f\sim\mathcal{D}}[|f(v)|]\leq 1+\epsilon
.

In
Theorem

3
we optimize the trade-off between the distortion
t
to the expected clan size
\mathbb{E}_{f\sim\mathcal{D}}[|f(v)|]
(in similar fashion to
Theorem

1
). Indeed, this trade-off in
Theorem

3
is asymptotically optimal
[
FL21
]
.
It is yet unclear what is the best possible hop-stretch obtainable with asymptotically optimal distortion-expected clan size trade-off.
In similar fashion to
Theorem

2
, in
Theorem

4
, we show that by increasing the distortion by a
\log\log n
factor, we can obtain a sub-logarithmic hop-stretch (while keeping the expected clan sizes the same). Further, in this version we can also drop the assumption that the aspect ratio is polynomial.

Theorem 4

(Clan embedding into ultrametric with small hop-stretch and arbitrary aspect-ratio)
.

Consider an
n
-vertex graph
G=(V,E,w)
, and parameters
k,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}\in[n]
.
Then there is a distribution
\mathcal{D}
over clan embeddings
(f,\chi)
into ultrametrics with hop-distortion
\left(O(k\cdot\log\log n),{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}O(k\cdot\log\log n)},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}\right)
, hop-path-distortion
\left(O(k\cdot\log n\cdot\log\log n),{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}\right)
, and such that for every point
v\in V
,
\mathbb{E}_{f\sim\mathcal{D}}[|f(v)|]\leq O(n^{\frac{1}{k}})
.

In addition, for every
\epsilon\in(0,1)
, there is distribution
\mathcal{D}
as above such that every
U\in\mathrm{supp}(\mathcal{D})
has hop-distortion
\left(O(\frac{\log n\cdot\log\log n}{\epsilon}),{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}O(\frac{\log n\cdot\log\log n}{\epsilon})},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}\right)
, hop-path-distortion
\left({O(\frac{\log^{2}n\cdot\log\log n}{\epsilon})},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}\right)
, and such that for every vertex
v\in V
,
\mathbb{E}_{f\sim\mathcal{D}}[|f(v)|]\leq 1+\epsilon
.

Our final embedding result is a one-to-many “subgraph preserving” embedding.
This is a strengthening of the path-distortion guarantee from
Theorems

3
and
4
. The path-distortion guarantee provides a bound only for
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}
-respecting paths, while in many applications we will not have such a promise.
A
path-tree one-to-many embedding
is a map
f:V\rightarrow 2^{T}
, where each vertex is sent to a subset of vertices of a tree
T
, and all the tree vertices are copies of some graph vertices
f(V)=T
(here for
A\subseteq V
,
f(A)=\cup_{v\in A}f(v)
).
In addition, each edge
e=\{u^{\prime},v^{\prime}\}
, where
u^{\prime}\in f(u),v^{\prime}\in f(v)
, is associated with a
u-v
path
P^{T}_{e}
in
G
of weight at most
w_{T}(e)
. Given a path
P
in
T
, we can obtain an associated path
P^{T}
in
G
by simply concatenating the associated paths of all the edges in
P
.
We say that
f
has hop-bound
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\beta}
if the length (in hops) of every such concatenation of associated paths
P^{T}
has at most
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\beta}
hops.
Note that
T
might contain edge of weight
\infty
.
Compared with the definition of clan embedding, here we embed into a tree (and not an ultrametric), there are no chiefs, and most importantly, here each edge in the tree is associated with a path in the graph. See
Definition

8
and
Figure

5
for a formal definition and an illustration.

In
Theorem

5
bellow, we obtain a path-tree one-to-many embedding
f
with hop-bound
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}O(\log^{3}n)\cdot h}
(for a parameter
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}
).
The important guarantee is the following: given a subgraph
H
of
G
, there is a subgraph
H^{\prime}
of
T
of weight at most
w_{T}(H^{\prime})\leq O(\log n)\cdot w(H)
, and such that for every pair of vertices
u,v
at hop-distance at most
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}
(w.r.t.
H
),
H^{\prime}
contains two copies
u^{\prime}\in f(u)
and
v^{\prime}\in f(v)
in the same connected component.
For compression, in the path-distortion guarantee we require (morally) that the entire subgraph
H
has hop-diameter
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}
, and get a single subgraph
H^{\prime}
of
T
containing a copy of each vertex in
H
. In contrast, here there is no limitation on
H
, and
H^{\prime}
provides a “localized” guarantee where near-by vertices (w.r.t. hops) should have copies in the same connected component in
H^{\prime}
. Note that this is a much stronger guarantee that can be used for problems where the solution does not necessarily has bounded hop-diameter (for example hop-constrained group Steiner forest).

Theorem 5

(Subgraph Preserving Embedding)
.

Consider an
n
-point graph
G=(V,E,w)
with polynomial aspect ratio, parameters
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h},k\in\mathbb{N}
and vertex
r\in V
.
Then there is a path-tree one-to-many embedding
f
of
G
into a tree
T
with hop bound
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}O(k\cdot\log^{3}n)\cdot h}
,
|f(V)|=(2n)^{1+\frac{1}{k}}
,
|f(r)|=1
, and such that for every sub-graph
H
, there is a subgraph
H^{\prime}
of
T
where for every
u,v\in H
with
\mathrm{hop}_{H}(u,v)\leq{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}
,
H^{\prime}
contains a pair of copies
u^{\prime}\in f(u)
and
v^{\prime}\in f(v)
in the same connected component. Further,
w_{T}(H^{\prime})\leq O(k\cdot\log n)\cdot w(H)
.

In
Theorem

11
we prove an alternative version of
Theorem

5
where the hop bound is
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\tilde{O}(k\cdot\log^{2}n)\cdot h}
and the bound on the weight of
H^{\prime}
is only
\tilde{O}(k\cdot\log n)\cdot w(H)
.
In the application of
Theorem

5
in our paper we will use
k=O(1)
as we want to minimize the hop and weight bounds, and the total number of copies is insignificant (as long as it is polynomial). Nevertheless, one can choose
k=\log n
to obtain linear number of copies in total (which might be desirable for efficient algorithms).

Haeupler
et al.
[
HHZ21b
]
had a similar embedding
to
Theorem

5
,
which they refer to as “copy tree embedding” (see theorem 8 in the full
arXiv version
).
Haeupler
et al.
[
HHZ21b
]
had hop bound of
O(\log^{3}n)
, and the cost of the corresponding copy of
H
was at most
O(\log^{3}n)\cdot w(H)
. Thus we get a significant polynomial savings in these aspects, which directly translates to the performance of our approximation algorithms.
An advantage of
[
HHZ21b
]
is that every vertex is guaranteed to have at most
O(\log n)
copies (implying
O(n\log n)
total copies), while our
Theorem

5
doesn’t have such a guarantee even for
k=\log n
, when the total number of copies is linear.
While such a guarantee could be useful for future applications, it is insignificant for the applications in this paper.

Our bicriteria approximation results.
H.V. stands for hop-constrained.
All approximation guarantees are in worst case, other than for the two online problems where the approximation is in expectation.
All input graphs are assumed to have polynomial (in
n
) aspect ratio.
The parameter
k
in online group Steiner tree/forest, denotes the size of an initial set
\mathcal{K}
containing all potential requests.

Our results for the group Steiner tree/forest, and online group Steiner tree/forest are based on our hop-constrained clan embedding. All the other results are application of our Ramsey type hop-constrained embedding, and obtained from
[
HHZ21b
]
in a black box manner.
The results marked with
(\checkmark)
match the state of the art approximation factor (when one ignores hop constrains).
Note that we obtain first improvement over the cost approximation to the well studied h.c.
k
-Steiner tree problems (since the 2011 conference version of
[
KS16
]
.)

[
HHZ21b
]
used their Ramsey type embeddings to obtain many different approximation algorithms on hop-constrained (abbreviated h.c.). Our improved Ramsey embeddings imply directly improved approximation factors, and hop-stretch, for all these problems.
Most prominently, we improve the cost approximation for the well studied h.c.
k
-Steiner tree problem
5
5

5

In the
k
-Steiner tree problem we are given a root
r\in V
, and a set
K\subseteq V
of at least
k
terminals. The goal is to find a connected subgraph
H
spanning the root
r
, and at least
k
terminals, of minimal weight. In the hop-constrained version we are additionally required to guarantee that the hop diameter of
H
will be at most
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}
.

over
[
KS16
]
(
[
KS16
]
approximation is superior to
[
HHZ21b
]
).
See
Corollary

3
and
Table

1
for a summary.

We go beyond applying our embeddings into
[
HHZ21b
]
as a black box and obtain further improvements.
A subgraph
H
of
G
is
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}
-respecting if for every
u,v\in V(H)
,
d^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(h)}}_{G}(u,v)\leq d_{H}(u,v)
.
In
Corollary

1
we show that there is a one-to-many embedding
f:V(G)\rightarrow 2^{T}
into a tree, such that for every connected
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}
-respecting subgraph
H
, there is a connected subgraph
H^{\prime}
of
T
of weight
O(\log n)\cdot w(H)
and hop diameter
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}O(\log^{2}n)\cdot h}
containing at least one vertex from
f(u)
for every
u\in V(H)
, see
Corollary

1
(alternatively, in
Corollary

2
we are guaranteed a similar subgraph of weight
\tilde{O}(\log n)\cdot w(H)
and hop diameter
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\tilde{O}(\log n)\cdot h}
).
We use
Corollary

1
to construct bicriteria approximation algorithm for the h.c. group Steiner tree problem, the cost of which matches the state of the art for the (non h.c.) group Steiner tree problem, thus answering
2
.
Later, we apply
Corollary

1
on the online h.c. group Steiner tree problem, and obtain competitive ratio that matches the competitive ratio non-constrained version (see
Theorem

13
).

Theorem 6
.

Given an instance of the
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}
-h.c. group Steiner tree problem with
k
groups on a graph with polynomial aspect ratio,
there is a poly-time algorithm that returns a subgraph
H
such that
w(H)\leq O(\log^{2}n\cdot\log k)\cdot\mathrm{OPT}
, and
\mathrm{hop}_{H}(r,g_{i})\leq{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}O(\log^{2}n)\cdot h}
for every
g_{i}
, where
\mathrm{OPT}
denotes the weight of the optimal solution.
Alternatively, one can return a subgraph
H
such that
w(H)\leq\tilde{O}(\log^{2}n\cdot\log k)\cdot\mathrm{OPT}
, and
\mathrm{hop}_{H}(r,g_{i})\leq{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\tilde{O}(\log n)\cdot h}
for every
g_{i}
.

Next we study the h.c. group Steiner forest problem, where we are given parameter
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}
, and
k
subset pairs
(S_{1},R_{1}),\dots,(S_{k},R_{k})\subseteq V
, where for every
i\in[k]
,
S_{i},R_{i}\subseteq V
. The goal is to construct a minimal weight forest
F
, such that for every
i
, there is a path with at most
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}
hops from a vertex in
S_{i}
to a vertex in
R_{i}
.
As the optimal solution to the h.c. group Steiner forest is not necessarily
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}
respecting, we cannot use
Corollary

1
. Instead, we are using our subgraph-preserving embeddings (
Theorems

5
and
11
) to obtain a bicriteria approximation. A similar result for the online h.c. group Steiner forest problem is presented in
Theorem

14
.

Theorem 7
.

Given an instance of the
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}
-h.c. group Steiner forest problem with
k
groups on a graph with polynomial aspect ratio, there is a poly-time algorithm that returns a subgraph
H
such that
w(H)\leq O(\log^{4}n\cdot\log k)\cdot\mathrm{OPT}
, and
\mathrm{hop}_{H}(S_{i},R_{i})\leq{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}O(\log^{3}n)\cdot h}
for every
i
, where
\mathrm{OPT}
denotes the weight of the optimal solution.
Alternatively, one can return a subgraph
H
such that
w(H)\leq\tilde{O}(\log^{4}n\cdot\log k)\cdot\mathrm{OPT}
, and
\mathrm{hop}_{H}(S_{i},R_{i})\leq{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\tilde{O}(\log^{2}n)\cdot h}
for every
i
.

1.1.3
Hop-constrained metric data structures

In metric data structures, our goal is to construct data structure that will store (estimated) metric distances compactly, and answer distance (or routing) queries efficiently.
As the distances returned by such data structure, do not have to respect the triangle inequality, one might hope to avoid any hop-stretch.
This is impossible in general. Consider a complete graph with edge weights sampled randomly from
\{1,\alpha\}
for some large
\alpha
. Clearly, from information theoretic considerations, in order to estimate
d_{G}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(1)}}
with arbitrarily large distortion (but smaller than
\alpha
), one must use
\Omega(n^{2})
space.
Therefore, in our metric data structures we will allow hop-stretch.

Distance Oracles.

A distance oracle is a succinct data structure
\mathsf{DO}
that (approximately) answers distance queries.
Chechik
[
Che15
]
(improving over previous results
[
TZ05
,
RTZ05
,
MN07
,
Wul13
,
Che14
]
) showed that any metric (or graph) with
n
points has a distance oracle of size
O(n^{1+\frac{1}{k}})
,
6
6

6

We measure size in machine words, each word is
\Theta(\log n)
bits.
that can report any distance in
O(1)
time with stretch at most
2k-1
(which is asymptotically optimal assuming Erdős girth conjecture
[
Erd64
]
). That is on query
u,v
, the answer
\mathsf{DO}(u,v)
satisfies

d_{G}(u,v)\leq\mathsf{DO}(u,v)\leq(2k-1)\cdot d_{G}(u,v)
.

Here we introduce the study of h.c. distance oracles. That is, given a parameter
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}
, we would like to construct a distance oracle that on query
u,v
, will return
d^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(h)}}(u,v)
, or some approximation of it.
Our result is the following:

Theorem 8

(Hop-constrained Distance Oracle)
.

For every weighted graph
G=(V,E,w)
on
n
vertices with polynomial aspect ratio, and parameters
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
\epsilon\in(0,1)
,
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}\in\mathbb{N}
, there is an efficiently constructible distance oracle
\mathsf{DO}
of size
O(n^{1+\frac{1}{k}}\cdot\log n)
, that for every distance query
(u,v)
, in
O(1)
time returns a value
\mathsf{DO}(u,v)
such that
d_{G}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(O(\frac{k\cdot\log\log n}{\epsilon})\cdot h)}}(u,v)\leq\mathsf{DO}(u,v)\leq(2k-1)(1+\epsilon)\cdot d_{G}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(h)}}(u,v)
.

Distance Labeling.

A
distance labeling
is a distributed version of a distance oracle. Given a graph
G=(V,E,w)
, each vertex
v
is assigned a label
\ell(v)
, and there is an algorithm
\mathcal{A}
, that given
\ell(u),\ell(v)
returns a value
\mathcal{A}(\ell(u),\ell(v))
approximating
d_{G}(u,v)
.
In their celebrated work, Thorup and Zwick
[
TZ05
]
constructed a distance labeling scheme with labels of size
O(n^{\frac{1}{k}}\cdot\log n)
,
6
and such that in
O(k)
time,
\mathcal{A}(\ell(u),\ell(v))
approximates
d_{G}(u,v)
within a
2k-1
factor.
We refer to
[
FGK23
]
for further details on distance labeling (see also
[
Pel00
,
GPPR04
,
EFN18
]
).

Matoušek
[
Mat96
]
showed that every metric space
(X,d)
could be embedded into
\ell_{\infty}
of dimension
O(n^{\frac{1}{k}}\cdot k\cdot\log n)
with distortion
2k-1
(for the case
k=O(\log n)
,
[
ABN11
]
later improved the dimension to
O(\log n)
).
As was previously observed, this embedding can serve as a labeling scheme (where the label of each vertex will be the representing vector in the embedding). From this point of view, the main contribution of
[
TZ05
]
is the small
O(k)
query time (as the label size/distortion tradeoff is similar).

Here we introduce the study of h.c. labeling schemes, where the goal is to approximate
d^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(h)}}_{G}(u,v)
.
Note that
d^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(h)}}_{G}
is not a metric function, and in particular does not embed into
\ell_{\infty}
with bounded distortion.
Hence there is no trivial labeling scheme which is embedding based.
Our contribution is the following:

Theorem 9

(Hop-constrained Distance Labeling)
.

For every weighted graph
G=(V,E,w)
on
n
vertices with polynomial aspect ratio, and parameters
k,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}\in\mathbb{N}
,
\epsilon\in(0,1)
, there is an efficient construction of a distance labeling that assigns each node
v
a label
\ell(v)
of size
O(n^{\frac{1}{k}}\cdot\log^{2}n)
, and such that there is an algorithm
\mathcal{A}
that on input
\ell(u),\ell(v)
, in
O(k)
time returns a value such that
d_{G}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(O(\frac{k\cdot\log\log n}{\epsilon})\cdot h)}}(v,u)\leq\mathcal{A}(\ell(v),\ell(u))\leq(2k-1)(1+\epsilon)\cdot d_{G}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(h)}}(v,u)
.

Compact Routing Scheme.

A
routing scheme
in a network is a mechanism that allows packets to be delivered from any node to any other node. The network is represented as a weighted undirected graph, and each node can forward incoming data by using local information stored at the node, called a
routing table
, and the (short) packet’s
header
. The routing scheme has two main phases: in the preprocessing phase, each node is assigned a routing table and a short
label
; in the routing phase, when a node receives a packet, it should make a local decision, based on its own routing table and the packet’s header (which may contain the label of the destination, or a part of it), of where to send the packet.
The
stretch
of a routing scheme is the worst-case ratio between the length of a path on which a packet is routed to the shortest possible path.
For fixed
k
, the state of the art is by Chechik
[
Che13
]
(improving previous works
[
PU89
,
ABLP90
,
AP92
,
Cow01
,
EGP03
,
TZ01
]
) who obtain stretch
3.68k
while using
O(k\cdot n^{\frac{1}{k}})
size tables and labels of size
O(k\cdot\log n)
.
6

For stretch
\Omega(\log n)
further improvements were obtained by Abraham
et al.
[
ACE+20
]
, and the Author and Le
[
FL21
]
.

Here we introduce the study of h.c. routing schemes, where the goal is to route the packet in a short path with small number of hops. Specially, for parameter
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}
, we are interested in a routing scheme that for every
u,v
will use a route with at most
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\beta\cdot h}
hops, and weight
\leq t\cdot d^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(h)}}_{G}(u,v)
, for some parameters
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\beta},t
. If
\mathrm{hop}_{G}(u,v)\geq{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}
, the routing scheme is not required to do anything, and any outcome will be accepted.
We answer
3
in the following:

Theorem 10

(Hop-constrained Compact Routing Scheme)
.

For every weighted graph
G=(V,E,w)
on
n
vertices with polynomial aspect ratio, and parameters
k,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}\in\mathbb{N}
,
\epsilon\in(0,1)
, there is an efficient construction of a compact routing scheme that assigns each node a table of size
O(n^{\frac{1}{k}}\cdot k\cdot\log n)
, label of size
O(k\cdot\log^{2}n)
. For every
u,v
such that
\mathrm{hop}_{G}(u,v)\leq{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}
, the routing of a packet from
u
to
v
, will be done using a path
P
such that
\mathrm{hop}(P)={\color[rgb]{1,0,0}O(\frac{k^{2}\cdot\log\log n}{\epsilon})\cdot h}
, and
w(P)\leq 3.68k\cdot(1+\epsilon)\cdot d_{G}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(h)}}(u,v)
.

1.2
Related work

Hop-constrained network design problems, and in particular routing, received considerable attention from the operations research community, see

[
WA88
,
Gou95
,
Gou96
,
Voß99
,
GR01
,
GM03
,
AT11
,
RAJ12
,
BFGP13
,
BHR13
,
BFG15
,
TCG15
,
DGM+16
,
Lei16
,
BF18
,
DMMY18
]
for a sample of papers.

Approximation algorithm for h.c. problems were previously constructed. They are usually considerably harder than their non h.c. counter parts, and often require for bicriteria approximation. Previously studied problems include minimum depth spanning tree
[
AFH+05
]
, degree bounded minimum diameter spanning tree
[
KLS05
]
, bounded depth Steiner tree
[
KP97
,
KP09
]
, h.c. MST
[
Rav94
]
, Steiner tree
[
MRS+98
]
, and
k
-Steiner tree
[
HKS09
,
KS16
]
. We refer to
[
HHZ21b
]
for further details.

Recently, Ghaffari
et al.
[
GHZ21
]
obtained a hop-constrained
\mathrm{poly}(\log)
competitive oblivious routing scheme using techniques based on
[
HHZ21b
]
hop-constrained Ramsey trees.
Notably, even though the
O(\frac{\log n}{\epsilon})
distortion in
Theorem

1
is superior to the
O(\frac{\log^{2}n}{\epsilon})
distortion in a similar theorem in
[
HHZ21b
]
,
[
HHZ21b
]
showed that the expected distortion in their embedding is only
\tilde{O}(\log\frac{n}{\epsilon})
. This additional property turned out to be important for the oblivious routing application. See further discussion in
Section

1.3
,
Question
4
.

Given a graph
G=(V,E,w)
, a
hop-set
is a set of edges
G^{\prime}
that when added to
G
,
d_{G\cup G^{\prime}}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(h)}}
well approximates
d_{G}
. Formally, an
(t,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h})
-hop-set is a set
G^{\prime}
such that
\forall u,v\in V
,
d_{G}(u,v)\leq d_{G\cup G^{\prime}}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(h)}}(u,v)\leq t\cdot d_{G}(u,v)
.
Elkin and Neiman
[
EN19
]
constructed
\left(1+\epsilon,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\left(\frac{\log k}{\epsilon}\right)^{\log k-2}}\right)
hop-sets with
O_{\epsilon,k}(n^{1+\frac{1}{k}})
edges.
Hop-sets were extensively studied, we refer to
[
EN20
]
for a survey.
Another related problem is
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}
-hop
t
-spanners. Here we are given a metric space
(X,d)
, and the goal is to construct a graph
G
over
X
such that
\forall x,y\in X
,
d_{X}(x,y)\leq d_{G}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(h)}}(x,y)\leq t\cdot d_{X}(x,y)
.
See
[
AMS94
,
Sol13
,
HIS13
,
LMS23
]
for Euclidean metrics,
[
FN22
]
for different metric spaces,
[
FL22a
,
FGN23
]
for reliable
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}
-hop spanners, and
[
ASZ20
]
for low-hop emulators (where
d_{G}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(O(\log\log n))}}
respects the triangle inequality).

The idea of one-to-many embedding of graphs was originated by Bartal and Mendel
[
BM04
]
, who for
k\geq 1
constructed embedding into ultrametric with
O(n^{1+\frac{1}{k}})
nodes and path distortion
O(k\cdot\log n\cdot\log\log n)
(see
Definition

8
, and ignore all hop constrains). The path distortion was later improved to
O(k\cdot\log n)

[
FL21
,
Bar21
]
.
Recently, Haeupler
et al.
[
HHZ21a
]
studied approximate copy tree embedding which is essentially equivalent to one-to-many tree embeddings. Their “path-distortion” is inferior:
O(\log^{2}n)
, however they were able to bound the number of copies of each vertex by
O(\log n)
in the worst case (not obtained by previous works).
One-to-many embeddings were also studied in the context of minor-free graphs, where Cohen-Addad
et al.
[
CFKL20
]
constructed embedding into low treewidth graphs with expected additive distortion. Later, the author and Le
[
FL21
]
also constructed clan, and Ramsey type, embeddings of minor free graph into low treewidth graphs.

Bartal
et al.
[
BFN19a
]
showed that even when the metric space is the shortest path metric of a planar graph with constant doubling dimension, the general metric Ramsey type embedding
[
MN07
]
cannot be substantially improved. Finally, there are also versions of Ramsey type embeddings
[
ACE+20
]
, and clan embeddings
[
FL21
]
into spanning trees of the input graph. This embeddings loses a
\log\log n
factor in the distortion compared with the embeddings into (non-spanning) trees.

1.3
Conclusion and open problems

Hop-constrained shortest path distances are very challenging to work with as they do not constitute a metric space. Haeupler, Hershkowitz, and Zuzic
[
HHZ21b
]
were to first to study metric embedding into trees of h.c. distances. While such a metric embedding is impossible in general, Haeupler
et al.
managed to do so by introducing hop-stretch (that is additional slack in the number of hops), and inclusion probability (that is Ramsey-type embedding). Haeupler
et al.
than used their Ramsey-type embedding to construct a “copy tree embedding” where each subgraph in the original graph has a corresponding copy in the image. Finally they used their embeddings to get a plethora of h.c. approximation and online algorithms.

Haeupler
et al.
constructions are based on stochastic decompositions ala
[
Bar96
]
. We employ more sophisticated metric embeddings techniques (based on
[
ACE+20
]
, which by itself is based on
[
MN07
]
) to obtain significantly better distortion and hop-stretch. We then employ ideas of clan embeddings
[
BM04
,
FL21
]
to obtain one-to-many embeddings with path distortion guarantees. Next, we use the clan embeddings to obtain path-tree subgraph-preserving embeddings, which are similar to
[
HHZ21b
]
copy tree embedding, while ours have a much superior guarantees. We then use our embeddings to improve the parameters for the plethora of h.c. approximation and online algorithms studied in
[
HHZ21b
]
(and previous papers).
Finally, we study h.c. metric data structures (not studied theoretically before). Surprisingly, using a combination of our metric embeddings (to obtain a rough estimate of the hop-constrained distance), together with previously known metric data structures (in a black-box fashion) we are able to construct h.c. metric data structure with parameters almost matching their non h.c. counterparts.
Our work leaves several open questions and directions:

1.

Metric embeddings of topologically restricted graphs: A large portion of the metric embeddings literature is concerned with metric embedding of topologically restricted graph families such as planar graphs, or fixed minor free graphs.
It will be interesting to understand how h.c. shortest path distances in such graphs behave, and to find additional structure that will allow for better metric embeddings.
While we do not expect to find better Ramsey type or clan embeddings into trees (as there is no such non h.c. counterparts), it is plausible that there are good metric embedding with low additive distortion into low treewidth graph. See
[
FKS19
,
CFKL20
,
FL21
,
FL22b
,
CCL+23
,
CLPP23
]
.

2.

Approximate Nearest Neighbor Search (ANNS): A very well studied metric data structure is that of ANNS (see the survey
[
AIR18
]
). While most of the previous work is on norm spaces, there have been also some work on general metrics
[
Fil23
]
and planar graphs
[
ACKW15
,
Fil23
]
. It will be interesting to see if these works can be extended to the case of h.c. distances.

3.

Optimizing the parameters in our h.c. Ramsey-type embeddings: The first question will be whether it is possible to remove the
O(\log\log n)
factors in
Theorem

2
(similarly in
Theorem

4
). Secondly, it will be interesting to optimize the distortion (hop-stretch) and remove the asymptotic notation there. Note that the
k
parameter governs the exponent in the inclusion probability, and hence is important.

4.

Expected distortion: Haeupler
et al.
[
HHZ21b
]
also studied the notion of expected distortion. Consider an ultrametric
U
, and a subset
M\subseteq V
of saved vertices. Given a pair of vertices
u,v\in V
, set
d_{U,M}(u,v)=\begin{cases}d_{U}(u,v)&u,v\in M\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}
.
The main result in
[
HHZ21b
]
is an embedding with inclusion probability
1-\epsilon
, distortion
O(\frac{\log^{2}n}{\epsilon})
, hop stretch
O(\frac{\log^{2}n}{\epsilon})
, and in addition, for every pair of vertices, the expected distortion is bounded by
\mathbb{E}_{U,M}\left[d_{U,M}(u,v)\right]\leq O(\log n\cdot\log(\frac{\log n}{\epsilon}))\cdot d_{G}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(h)}}(u,v)
. Note that for inclusion probability
1-\epsilon
, our
Theorem

1
only guarantees distortion
O(\frac{\log n}{\epsilon})
(in the worst case). Thus
[
HHZ21b
]
relaxation for expected distortion improves the dependence on
\epsilon
exponentially. As it turns out, in the application of
[
HHZ21b
]
for oblivious routing (see
[
GHZ21
]
) one needs inclusion probability
1-\epsilon
for
\epsilon=\mathrm{poly}(\frac{1}{n})
, thus this difference is significant. It will be interesting to optimize the expected distortion w.r.t. the other parameters using the more sophisticated tools in this paper.

5.

H.c. Ramsey type embeddings into spanning trees (subgraph): Our embeddings in this paper are mostly into ultrametrics (or into abstract non-subgraph trees in
Theorems

5
and
11
). For non h.c. distances, stochastic, Ramsey-type, and clan embeddings were studied into spanning (sub-graph) trees as well
[
EEST08
,
AN19
,
ACE+20
,
FL21
]
. It interesting to see if it is possible to obtain similar results for h.c. distance (perhaps by allowing the distances in the tree to be also h.c.).

6.

Relaxed notions of distortion: In the metric embeddings literature, there are many works studying relaxed notions of distortion such as scaling distortion
[
ABC+05
,
CDG06
,
ABN11
,
BFN19b
]
(where
1-\epsilon
of the pairs guaranteed to have distortion
f(\frac{1}{\epsilon})
), terminal distortion
[
EFN17
,
EN21
,
MMMR18
,
NN19
,
CN21
]
(where there is distortion guarantee only for the pairs
K\times V
for a subsets
K\subseteq V
of terminals), and prioritized distortion
[
EFN18
,
BFN19b
,
EN22
,
FGK23
]
(where there is a priority order over the vertices, and the distortion guarantee is w.r.t. the order). It will be interesting to construct metric embeddings of h.c. distance with refined notions of distortion.

2
Preliminaries

\tilde{O}
notation hides poly-logarithmic factors, that is
\tilde{O}(g)=O(g)\cdot\mathrm{polylog}(g)
.
Consider an undirected weighted graph
G=(V,E,w)
, and hop parameter
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}
.
A graph is called unweighted if all its edges have unit weight.
In general, in the input graphs we will assume that all weights are finite positive numbers, while in our output graphs we will also use
\infty
as a weight (this will represent that either vertices are disconnected or every path between them have more than
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}
hops).
We denote
G
’s vertex set and edge set by
V(G)
and
E(G)
, respectively. Often we will abuse notation and write
G
instead of
V(G)
.

d_{G}
denotes the shortest path metric in
G
, i.e.,
d_{G}(u,v)
equals to the minimal weight of a path between
u
to
v
.
A path
P=(v_{0},v_{1},\dots,v_{h})
is said to have
\mathrm{hop}(P)={\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}
hops. The
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}
-hop distance between two vertices
u,v
is

d_{G}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(h)}}(u,v)=\min\{w(P)\penalty\ \mid\penalty\ P\mbox{ is a path between $u$ and $v$ with }\mathrm{hop}(P)\leq{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}\}\penalty\ .

If there is no path from
u
to
v
with at most
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}
hops, then
d_{G}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(h)}}(u,v)=\infty
.

\mathrm{hop}_{G}(u,v)=\min\left\{h\mid d_{G}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(h)}}(u,v)<\infty\right\}
is the minimal number of hops in a
u
-
v
path. For two subsets
S,T\subseteq V
,
\mathrm{hop}_{G}(S,T)=\min_{s\in S,t\in T}\left\{\mathrm{hop}_{G}(s,t)\right\}
, for a vertex
v
, we can also write
\mathrm{hop}_{G}(v,S)=\mathrm{hop}_{G}(\{v\},S)=\min_{s\in S}\left\{\mathrm{hop}_{G}(v,s)\right\}
.
The
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}
-hop diameter of the graph is

\mathrm{diam}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(h)}}(G)=\max\{d_{G}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(h)}}(u,v)\}

the maximal
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}
-hop distance between a pair of vertices. Note that in many cases, this will be
\infty
.

B_{G}(v,r)=\{u\mid d_{G}(u,v)\leq r\}
is the closed ball around
u
of radius
r
, and similarly
B^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(h)}}_{G}(v,r)=\{u\mid d^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(h)}}_{G}(u,v)\leq r\}
is the
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}
-hop-constrained ball.

Throughout the paper we will assume that the minimal weight edge in the input graph has weight
1
, note that due to scaling this assumption do not lose generality.
The
aspect ratio
,
3
is the ratio between the maximal to minimal weight in
G

\frac{\max_{e\in E}w(e)}{\min_{e\in E}w(e)}
,
or simply
\max_{e\in E}w(e)
as we assumed the the minimal distance is
1
.
In many places (similarly to
[
HHZ21b
]
) we will assume that the aspect ratio is polynomial in
n
(actually in all results other than
Theorems

2
and
4
). This assumption will always be stated explicitly.

For a subset
A\subseteq V
of vertices,
G[A]=(A,E_{A}=E\cap{V\choose 2},w_{\upharpoonright E_{A}})
denotes the subgraph induced by
A
.
Similarly, for a subset of edges
F\subset E
, which are incident on
A\subseteq V
, the induced subgraph is
G[F]=(A,F,w_{\upharpoonright F})
. We consider here a path as a subset of edges.
The diameter of a cluster
S\subseteq V
, denoted by
\mathrm{diam}(S)
is
\max_{u,v\in S}d_{G[S]}(u,v)
.

7
7

7

This is often called
strong
diameter. A related notion is the
weak
diameter of a cluster
S
, defined
\max_{u,v\in S}d_{G}(u,v)
. Note that for a metric space, weak and strong diameter are equivalent. See
[
Fil19a
]
.

Similarly, for a subset of edges
\tilde{E}\subseteq E
, where
A\subseteq V
is the subset of vertices in
\cup_{e\in\tilde{E}}e
, the graph induced by
\tilde{E}
is
G[\tilde{E}]=(A,\tilde{E},w_{\upharpoonright\tilde{E}})
.

An ultrametric
\left(Z,d\right)
is a metric space satisfying a
strong form of the triangle inequality, that is, for all
x,y,z\in Z
,

d(x,z)\leq\max\left\{d(x,y),d(y,z)\right\}
.
In
Definition

1
we define a hierarchically well-separated tree (HST). It is well known that HST’s and ultrametrics are equivalent (see
[
BLMN05b
]
), and we will use these two terms interchangeably.

Definition 1

(Hierarchically well-separated tree - HST)
.

An HST is a metric space
\left(Z,d\right)
whose elements
are the leaves of a rooted labeled tree
T
. Each
z\in T
is associated
with a label
\ell\left(z\right)\geq 0
such that if
q\in T
is a
descendant of
z
then
\ell\left(q\right)\leq\ell\left(z\right)

and
\ell\left(q\right)=0
iff
q
is a leaf. The distance between
leaves
z,q\in Z
is defined as
d_{T}(z,q)=\ell\left(\mbox{lca}\left(z,q\right)\right)

where
\mbox{lca}\left(z,q\right)
is the least common ancestor of

z
and
q
in
T
.

Metric Embeddings

Classically, a metric embedding is defined as a function
f:X\rightarrow Y
between the points of two metric spaces
(X,d_{X})
and
(Y,d_{Y})
.
A metric embedding
f
is said to be
dominating
if for every pair of points
x,y\in X
, it holds that
d_{X}(x,y)\leq d_{Y}(f(x),f(y))
.
The distortion of a dominating embedding
f
is
\max_{x\not=y\in X}\frac{d_{Y}(f(x),f(y))}{d_{X}(x,y)}
. Here we will also study a more permitting generalization of metric embedding introduced by Cohen-Addad
et al.
[
CFKL20
]
, which is called
one-to-many
embedding.

Definition 2

(One-to-many embedding)
.

A
one-to-many embedding
is a function
f:X\rightarrow 2^{Y}
from the points of a metric space
(X,d_{X})
into non-empty subsets of points of a metric space
(Y,d_{Y})
, where the subsets
\{f(x)\}_{x\in X}
are disjoint. For a point
x^{\prime}\in Y
,

f^{-1}(x^{\prime})
denotes the unique point
x\in X
such that
x^{\prime}\in f(x)
. If no such point exists,
f^{-1}(x^{\prime})=\emptyset
.
A point
x^{\prime}\in f(x)
is called a
copy
of
x
, while
f(x)
is called the
clan
of
x
.
For a subset
A\subseteq X
of vertices, denote
f(A)=\cup_{x\in A}f(x)
.
We say that
f
is
dominating
if for every pair of points
x,y\in X
, it holds that
d_{X}(x,y)\leq\min_{x^{\prime}\in f(x),y^{\prime}\in f(y)}d_{Y}(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})
.

Here we will study the new notion of clan embeddings introduced by the author and Le
[
FL21
]
.

Definition 3

(Clan embedding)
.

A clan embedding from metric space
(X,d_{X})
into a metric space
(Y,d_{Y})
is a pair
(f,\chi)
where
f:X\rightarrow 2^{Y}
is a dominating one-to-many embedding, and
\chi:X\rightarrow Y
is a classic embedding, where for every
x\in X
,
\chi(x)\in f(x)
.
\chi(x)
is referred to as the chief of the clan of
x
(or simply the chief of
x
).
We say that the clan embedding
f
has distortion
t
if for every

x,y\in X
,
\min_{y^{\prime}\in f(y)}d_{Y}(y^{\prime},\chi(x))\leq t\cdot d_{X}(x,y)
.

Bounded hop distances

We say that an embedding
f:V\rightarrow X
has distortion
t
, and
hop-stretch

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\beta}
, if for every
u,v\in V
it holds that

d_{G}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(\beta h)}}(u,v)\leq d_{X}(f(u),f(v))\leq t\cdot d_{G}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(h)}}(u,v)\penalty\ .

[
HHZ21b
]
showed an example of a graph
G
where in every classic embedding of
G
into a metric space, either the hop-stretch
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\beta}
, or the distortion
t
must be polynomial in
n
. In particular, there is no hope for a classic embedding (or even stochastic) with both hop-stretch and distortion being sub-polynomial.

We will therefore study hop-constrained clan embeddings, and Ramsey type embeddings.

Definition 4

(Hop-distortion of Ramsey type embedding)
.

An embedding
f
from a weighted graph
G=(V,E,w)
to metric space
(X,d_{X})
has Ramsey hop distortion
(t,M,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\beta},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h})
if
M\subseteq V
, for every
u,v\in V
it holds that
d_{G}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(\beta\cdot h)}}(u,v)\leq d_{X}(f(v),f(u))
, and for every
u\in V
and
v\in M
,
d_{X}(f(v),f(u))\leq t\cdot d_{G}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(h)}}(u,v)
.

Definition 5

(Hop-distortion of clan embedding)
.

A clan embedding
(f,\chi)
from a weighted graph
G=(V,E,w)
to a metric space
(X,d_{X})
has hop distortion
(t,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\beta},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h})
if for every
u,v\in V
, it holds that
d_{G}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(\beta\cdot h)}}(u,v)\leq\min_{v^{\prime}\in f(v),u^{\prime}\in f(u)}d_{X}(v^{\prime},u^{\prime})
, and
\min_{v^{\prime}\in f(v)}d_{X}(v^{\prime},\chi(u))\leq t\cdot d_{G}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(h)}}(u,v)
.

The following definitions will be useful to argue that the clan embedding preserves properties of subgraphs, and not only vertex pairs.

Definition 6

(
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}
-respecting)
.

A subgraph
H
of
G
is
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}
-
respecting
if for every
u,v\in H
it holds that
d^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(h)}}_{G}(u,v)\leq d_{H}(u,v)
. Often we will abuse notation and say that a path
P
is
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}
-
respecting
, meaning that the subgraph induced by the path is
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}
-respecting.

Definition 7

(Hop-path-distortion)
.

We say that the one-to-many embedding
f:V\to Y
has
hop path distortion

(t,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h})
if for every
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}
-respecting path
P=\left(v_{0},v_{1},\dots,v_{m}\right)
there are vertices
v^{\prime}_{0},\dots,v^{\prime}_{m}
where
v^{\prime}_{i}\in f(v_{i})
such that
\sum_{i=0}^{m-1}d_{Y}(v^{\prime}_{i},v^{\prime}_{i+1})\leq t\cdot\sum_{i=0}^{m-1}w(v_{i},v_{i+1})
.

3
Paper overview

3.1
Ramsey type embeddings

Previous approach.

The construction of Ramsey type embeddings in
[
HHZ21b
]
is based on padded decompositions such as in
[
Bar96
,
Fil19a
]
. Specifically, Haeupler
et al.
show that for every parameter
\Delta
, one can partition the vertices into clusters
\mathcal{C}
such that for every
C\in\mathcal{C}
,
\mathrm{diam}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(O(\log^{2}n)\cdot h)}}(C)=\max_{u,v\in C}d_{G}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(O(\log^{2}n)\cdot h)}}(u,v)\leq\Delta
and every ball
B^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(h)}}(v,r)=\{u\mid d^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(h)}}_{G}(u,v)\leq r\}
, for
r=\Omega(\frac{\Delta}{\log^{2}n})
, belongs to a single cluster (i.e.
v
is “padded”) with probability
\approx 1-\frac{1}{\log n}
.

[
HHZ21b
]
then recursively partitions the graph to create clusters with geometrically decreasing diameter
\Delta
. The resulting hierarchical partition defines an ultrametric, where the vertices that are padded in all the levels belong to the set
M
. By union bound each vertex belong to
M
with probability at least
\frac{1}{2}
, and the distortion and hop-stretch equal to the parameters of the decomposition:
O(\log^{2}n)
.

Our goal here is to improve the different parameters: stretch, hop-stretch, and inclusion probability. We begin by optimizing the trade-off between stretch to inclusion probability (which mostly resembles classic non hop-constrained work). Then we will further optimize the hop-stretch (by introducing small slack to the stretch).

Theorem

1
: optimal distortion - inclusion probability tradeoff.

We use a more sophisticated approach, similar to previous works on clan embeddings
[
FL21
]
, and deterministic construction of Ramsey trees
[
ACE+20
]
.
The main task is to prove a “distributional” version of
Theorem

1
. Specifically, given a parameter
k
, and a measure
\mu:X\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 1}
, we construct a Ramsey type embedding with Ramsey hop-distortion
(16k,M,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}O(k\cdot\log n)},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h})
such that
\mu(M)\geq\mu(X)^{1-\frac{1}{k}}
(
Lemma

1
).
Theorem

1
then follows using the minimax theorem.
The construction of the distributional
Lemma

1
is
a deterministic recursive ball growing algorithm.
Our algorithm closely resembles the Ramsey (non-spanning) trees construction from
[
ACE+20
]
(algorithm 1 in the Journal version). In particularity, if one sets
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}=\infty
, up to some technical differences, it will be the same algorithm.

Given a cluster
X
and scale parameter
2^{i}
, we partition
X
into clusters
X_{1},\dots,X_{s}
such that each
X_{i}
is contained in a hop bounded ball
B_{G[X]}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(i\cdot O(k)\cdot h)}}(v,2^{i-1})
.
Then we recursively construct a Ramsey type embedding for each cluster
X_{q}
(with scale
2^{i-1}
), and combine the obtained ultrametrics using a new root with label
2^{i}
.
Note that there is a dependence between the radius of the balls, to the number of hops they are allowed. Specifically, in each level we decrease the radius by multiplicative factor of
2
, and the number of hops by an additive factor of
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h^{\prime}=O(k)\cdot h}
.
We maintain a set of active vertices
M\subseteq X
. Initially all the vertices are active. An active vertex
v\in M
will cease to be active if the ball
B_{G}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(h)}}(v,\Theta(\frac{2^{i}}{k}))
intersects with more than one cluster. The set of vertices that remain active till the end of the algorithm, e.g. vertices that were “padded” in every level, will constitute the set
M
in
Lemma

1
.
The “magic” happens in the creation of the clusters (
Algorithm

3
), so that to ensure that large enough portion of the vertices remain active till the end of the algorithm.
On an (inaccurate and) intuitive level, inductively, at level
i-1
, the “probability” of a vertex
v
to be padded in all future levels is
\left(|B^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(i\cdot h^{\prime})}}(v,2^{i-3})|\right)^{-\frac{1}{k}}
.
By a ball growing argument,
v
is padded in the
i
’th level with “probability”
\left(\frac{|B^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}((i+1)\cdot h^{\prime})}}(v,2^{i-2})|}{|B^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(i\cdot h^{\prime})}}(v,2^{i-3})|}\right)^{-\frac{1}{k}}
. Hence by the induction hypothesis,
v
is padded at level
i
and all the future levels with probability
\left(|B^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}((i+1)\cdot h^{\prime})}}(v,2^{i-2})|\right)^{-\frac{1}{k}}
. Note that for this cancellations to work out, we are paying an
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h^{\prime}=O(k\cdot h)}
additive factor in the hop-stretch in each level, as we need that the “minimum possible cluster” (among the possibly considered clusters, minimum w.r.t. containment) at the current level will contain the “maximum possible cluster” (among the possibly considered clusters, maximum w.r.t. containment) in the next one.
In other words, consider and
i
-level cluster
X
which contains an
i-1
-level cluster
Y
.
X
and
Y
were chosen from a set of
O(k)
nested clusters
X\in\left\{A^{X}_{0}\subseteq A^{X}_{1}\subseteq\cdots\subseteq A^{X}_{2k}\right\}
, and
Y\in\left\{A^{Y}_{0}\subseteq A^{Y}_{1}\subseteq\cdots\subseteq A^{Y}_{2k}\right\}
. Our analysis requires that
A^{Y}_{2k}\subseteq A^{X}_{0}
(regardless of the
A^{Y}_{j}
implemented).

Theorem

2
:
\log\log n
hop-stretch.

The construction of the embedding for
Theorem

2
is the same as that of
Theorem

1
in all aspects other than the cluster creation. In particular, we first prove a distributional lemma (
Lemma

4
) and use the minimax theorem.
The analysis of the algorithm of
Theorem

1
required a very strong “containment of potential created clusters” guarantee (
A^{Y}_{2k}\subseteq A^{X}_{0}
above).
This dependence between the different scale levels contributes an additive factor of
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}O(kh)}
to the hop allowance per level. Thus for polynomial aspect ratio it has
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}O(k\cdot\log n)}
hop-stretch.

To decrease the hop-stretch, here we create clusters w.r.t. “hop diameter” at most
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}O(k\cdot\log\log n)\cdot h}
, regardless of the current scale.
As a result, it is harder to relate between the different levels (as the “maximum possible cluster” in the next level is not contained in the “minimum possible one” in the current level).
To compensate for that, instead, we use a stronger per-level guarantees.
A similar phenomena where one cannot directly compare consecutive levels also appeared in the spanning (sub-graph) Ramsey trees of Abraham
et al.
[
ACE+20
]
(see also
[
AN19
,
FL21
]
). In particular, our algorithm for
Theorem

2
is morally similar to their algorithm 4 (in the journal version). However, as
[
ACE+20
]
implimented their algorithm in the petal decomposition framework, the technicalities are quite different.
An additional advantage of this approach is that it holds for graphs with unbounded aspect ratio (as the hop-stretch is unrelated to the number of scales).

In more detail, at level
i
we create a ball cluster inside
B^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(O(k\cdot\log\log n)\cdot h)}}(v,2^{i-2})
, however instead of using the ball growing argument on the entire possibilities spectrum, we use it only in a smaller “strip”, loosing a
\log\log n
factor, but obtaining that the probability of
v
to be padded in the current
i
level is
\left(\frac{|B^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(O(k\cdot\log\log n)\cdot h)}}(v,2^{i-2})|}{|X|}\right)^{\frac{1}{k}}
. Inductively we assume that the “probability” of a vertex
v\in X
to be padded in all the levels is
|X|^{-\frac{1}{k}}
. As the cardinality of the resulting cluster is smaller than
\left|B^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(O(k\cdot\log\log n)\cdot h)}}(v,2^{i-2})\right|
, the probability that
v
is padded in all the levels (including
i
) is indeed
|X|^{-\frac{1}{k}}
.

3.2
Clan embeddings

The construction of the clan embeddings
Theorems

3
and
4
are similar to the Ramsey type embeddings. In particular, in both cases the key step is a distributional lemma (
Lemmas

7
and
11
), where the algorithms for the distributional lemmas are deterministic ball growing algorithms.
The main difference, is that while in the Ramsey type embeddings we partition the graph into clusters,
in the clan embedding we create a cover.
Specifically, given a cluster
X
and scale
2^{i}
, the created cover is a collection of clusters
X_{1},\dots,X_{s}
such that each
X_{i}
is contained in a hop bounded ball
B_{G[X]}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(O(i\cdot k)\cdot h)}}(v,2^{i-2})
, and every ball
B_{G[X]}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(h)}}(u,\Omega(\frac{2^{i}}{k}))
is fully contained in some cluster. Each vertex
u
might belong to several clusters.
In the Ramsey type embedding our goal was to minimize the measure of the “close to the boundary” vertices, as they were deleted from the set
M
.
On the other hand, here the goal is to bound the combined multiplicity of all the vertices (w.r.t. the measure
\mu
), which governs the clan sizes.

Path distortion and subgraph preserving embedding

An additional property of the clan embedding is path distortion.
Specifically, given an (
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}
-respecting) path
P=\left(v_{0},v_{1},\dots,v_{m}\right)
in
G
, we are guaranteed that there are vertices
v^{\prime}_{0},\dots,v^{\prime}_{m}
where
v^{\prime}_{i}\in f(v_{i})
such that
\sum_{i=0}^{m-1}d_{U}(v^{\prime}_{i},v^{\prime}_{i+1})\leq O(k\cdot\log n)\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{m-1}w(v_{i},v_{i+1})
.
The proof of the path distortion property (
Lemma

9
) is recursive and follow similar lines to
[
BM04
]
.
Specifically, for a scale
2^{i}
we iteratively partition the path vertices
v_{0},v_{1},\dots,v_{m}
into the clusters
X_{1},\dots,X_{s}
, while minimizing the number of “split edges”: consecutive vertices assigned to different clusters. We inductively construct “copies” for each sub-path internal to a cluster
X_{j}
. The “split edges” are used as an evidence that the path
P
has weight comparable to the scale
2^{i}
times the number of split edges, and thus we could afforded them (as the maximal price of an edge in the ultrametric is
2^{i}
).

Next, we construct the subgraph preserving embedding. Specifically, we construct a clan embedding
f:V\rightarrow 2^{T}
, such that all the vertices in
T
are copies of
G
vertices:
V(T)=\cup_{v\in V}f(v)
, and every edge
e=\{u^{\prime},v^{\prime}\}\in T
for
u^{\prime}\in f(u)
,
v^{\prime}\in f(v)
is associated with a path
P_{e}
from
u
to
v
in
G
of weight
\leq d_{T}(u^{\prime},v^{\prime})
. Concatenating edge paths we obtain an induced path for every pair of vertices
u^{\prime},v^{\prime}
in
T
.
In
Corollary

1
we construct such subgraph preserving embedding, where the number of hops in every induced path is bounded by
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}O(\log^{2}n)\cdot h}
, and such that for every connected (
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}
-respecting) subgraph
H
of
G
, there is a connected subgraph
H^{\prime}
of
T
of weight
w_{T}(H^{\prime})\leq O(\log n)\cdot w_{G}(H)
, where for every
v\in H
,
H^{\prime}
contains some vertex from
f(v)
. In
Corollary

2
we obtain an alternative tradeoff where the number of hops in every induced path is bounded by
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\tilde{O}(\log n)\cdot h}
, and the weight of the subgraph
H^{\prime}
is bounded by
w_{T}(H^{\prime})\leq\tilde{O}(\log n)\cdot w_{G}(H)
.

Finally, we obtain a “subgraph-preserving embedding” for arbitrary (that is not necessarily
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}
-respecting) subgraphs (
Theorems

5
and
11
).
Specifically, given arbitrary subgraph
H
of
G
, we are guarantying that
T
will contain a (a not necessarily connected) subgraph
H^{\prime}
of
T
of weight at most
w_{T}(H^{\prime})\leq O(\log n)\cdot w_{G}(H)
, such that for every pair of points
u,v
where
\mathrm{hop}_{H}(u,v)\leq{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}
,
H^{\prime}
will contain two vertices from
f(u)
and
f(v)
in the same connected component.

A key component in the proof of
Theorem

5
is a construction of a
sparse cover
(
Lemma

16
). Here we are given a parameter
\Delta
, and partition the graph into clusters of diameter
O(\log n)\cdot\Delta
, such that every ball of radius
\Delta
is contained in some cluster, and the total sum of weights of all the cluster induced subgraphs is at most constant times larger than the weight of
G
.

3.3
Approximation algorithms

Following the approach of
[
HHZ21b
]
, our improved Ramsey type embeddings (
Theorems

1
and
2
) imply improved approximation algorithms for all the problems studied in
[
HHZ21b
]
. We refer to
[
HHZ21b
]
for details, and to
Corollary

3
(and
Table

1
) for summary.

For the group Steiner tree problem (and its online version), using the subgraph preserving embedding, we are able to obtain a significant improvement beyond the techniques of
[
HHZ21b
]
. In fact, the cost of our approximation algorithm matches the state of the art for the non hop-restricted case.
Our approach is as follows: first we observe that the optimal solution
H
for the h.c. group Steiner tree problem is a tree, and hence
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2h}
-respecting. It follows that a copy
H^{\prime}
of the optimal solution
H
(of weight
O(\log n)\cdot\mathrm{OPT}
) could be found in the image
T
of the subgraph preserving embedding
f
. Next we use known (non h.c.) approximation algorithms for the group Steiner tree problem on trees
[
GKR00
]
to find a valid solution
H^{\prime\prime}
in
T
of weight
O(\log^{2}n\cdot\log k)\cdot\mathrm{OPT}
. Finally, by combining the associated paths we obtain an induced solution in
G
of the same cost. The bound on the hops in the induced paths insures an
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}O(\log^{2}n)}
hop-stretch.
The competitive algorithm for the online group Steiner tree problem follows similar lines.
A similar approach is then used on h.c. group Steiner forest (and its online version). However, a the optimal solution is not necessarily
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}
-respecting, we use
Theorem

5
instead of
Corollary

1
.

3.4
Hop-constrained metric data structures

At first glance, as hop-constrained distances are non-metric, constructing metric data structures for them seems to be very challenging, and indeed, the lack of any previous work on this very natural problem serves as an evidence.

Initially, using our Ramsey type embedding we construct distance labelings in a similar fashion to the distance labeling scheme of
[
MN07
]
: construct
O(n^{\frac{1}{k}}\cdot k)
Ramsey type embedding into ultrametrics
U_{1},U_{2},\dots
such that
U_{i}
is ultrametric over
V
with the set
M_{i}
of saved vertices. It will hold that every vertex belongs to some set
M_{i}
.
For trees (and ultrametrics) there are very efficient distance labeling schemes (constant size and query time, and
1.5
-distortion
[
FGNW17
]
).
We define the label of each vertex
v
to be the union of the labels for the ultrametrics, and the index
i
of the ultrametric where
v\in M_{i}
.
As a result we obtain a distance labeling with label size
O(n^{\frac{1}{k}}\cdot k)
, constant query time, and such that for every
u,v
,
d^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(O(k\cdot\log\log n)\cdot h)}}_{G}(u,v)\leq\mathcal{A}(\ell(v),\ell(u))\leq O(k\cdot\log\log n)\cdot d^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(h)}}_{G}(u,v)
.
While the space, query time, and hop-stretch are quite satisfactory, the distortion leaves much to be desired.

To obtain an improvement, our next step is to construct a data structures for a fixed scale. Specifically, for every scale
2^{i}
, we construct a graph
G_{i}
such that for every pair
u,v
where
d_{G}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(h)}}(u,v)\approx 2^{i}
, it holds that
d_{G}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(O(\frac{k\cdot\log\log n}{\epsilon})\cdot h)}}(u,v)\leq d_{G_{i}}(u,v)\leq(1+\epsilon)\cdot(2k-1)\cdot d_{G}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(h)}}(u,v)
.
Thus, if we were to know that
d_{G}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(h)}}(u,v)\approx 2^{i}
, then we could simply use state of the art distance labeling for
G_{i}
, ignoring hop constrains. Now, using our first Ramsey based distance labeling scheme we can obtain the required coarse approximation!
Our final construction consist of Ramsey based distance labeling, and in addition state of the art distance labeling
[
TZ05
]
for the graphs
G_{i}
for all possible distance scales. Given a query
u,v
, we first coarsely approximate
d_{G}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(h)}}(u,v)
to obtain some value
2^{i}
. Then we returned the answer from the distance labeling scheme that was prepared in advance for
G_{i}
.
Our approach for distance oracles, and compact routing scheme is similar.

4
Hop-constrained Ramsey type embedding

First, we will prove a “distributional” version of
Theorem

1
. That is, we will receive a distribution
\mu
over the points, and deterministically construct a single Ramsey type embedding such that
|M|
will be large. Later, we will use the Minimax theorem to conclude
Theorem

1
.
We begin with some definitions: a
measure
over a finite set
X
, is simply a function
\mu:X\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
. The measure of a subset
A\subseteq X
, is
\mu(A)=\sum_{x\in A}\mu(x)
.
We say that
\mu
is a
probability measure
if
\mu(X)=1
. We say that
\mu
is a
(\geq 1)
-measure
if for every
x\in X
,
\mu(x)\geq 1
.

Lemma 1
.

Consider an
n
-vertex weighted graph
G=(V,E,w)
with polynomial aspect ratio,
(\geq 1)
-measure
\mu:V\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 1}
,
integer parameters
k\geq 1
,
h\in[n]
and subset
\mathcal{M}\subseteq V
.
Then there is a Ramsey type embedding into an ultrametric
U
with Ramsey hop distortion

(16k,M,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}O(k\cdot\log n)},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h})
where
M\subseteq\mathcal{M}
and
\mu(M)\geq\mu(\mathcal{M})^{1-\frac{1}{k}}
.

Note that
Lemma

1
guarantees that for every
u,v\in V
,
d_{G}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(O(k\cdot\log n)\cdot h)}}(u,v)\leq d_{U}(u,v)
. Further, if
u\in M
, then
d_{U}(u,v)\leq 16k\cdot d_{G}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(h)}}(u,v)
.
The freedom to choose
\mathcal{M}
will be useful in our construction of metric data structures (but will not be used during the proof of
Theorem

1
).
The proof of
Lemma

1
is deferred to
Section

4.1
. Now we will prove
Theorem

1
using
Lemma

1
.

Proof of
Theorem

1
.

We can assume that
k<n
(and also
\epsilon>\frac{1}{n}
), as otherwise we are allowed
\Omega(n)
hops and stretch. Thus we can ignore the hop constrains, and
Theorem

1
will simply follow from classic Ramsey type embeddings (e.g.
[
MN07
]
).

We begin with an auxiliary claim, which translates from the language of
(\geq 1)
-measures used in
Lemma

1
to that of probability measures (while increasing the distortion from
16k
to
16(k+1)
).

Claim 1
.

Consider an
n
-vertex weighted graph
G=(V,E,w)
with polynomial aspect ratio, probability-measure
\mu:V\rightarrow[0,1]
,
