\left({O(k\cdot\log\mu(V)\cdot\log\log\mu(V))},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}\right)
, and
such that
\mathbb{E}_{v\sim\mu}[|f(v)|]\leq\mu(V)^{1+\frac{1}{k}}
.

The proof of
Theorem

4
using
Lemma

11
follows the exact same lines as the proof of
Theorem

3
using
Lemma

7
, and thus we will skip it.

5.3
Proof of
Lemma

11
: alternative distributional h.c. clan embedding

For the embedding of
Lemma

11
we will use the exact same
Hop-constrained-clan-embedding
(
Algorithm

5
), with the only difference that instead of using the
Clan-create-Cluster
procedure (
Algorithm

7
), we will use the
Clan-create-cluster-alt
procedure (
Algorithm

8
).
Similarly to the proof of
Lemma

7
we will assume that
\mathrm{diam}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(h)}}(G)<\infty
. The exact same argument from
Section

4.1.1
can be used to remove this assumption.
We begin by making the call
Hop-constrained-clan-embedding
(G[V],\mu,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h},k,2^{\phi})
, where the call to
Clan-create-Cluster
in
algorithm

6
of
Algorithm

6
is replaced by a call to
Clan-create-cluster-alt
.

input :
Induced graph
G[Y]
, parameters
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h},k\in\mathbb{N}
,
\geq 1
-measure
\mu
, scale
i
.

output :
Three clusters
(\underline{A},A,\overline{A})
, where
\underline{A}\subseteq A\subseteq\overline{A}
.

set
L=\lceil 1+\log\log\mu(Y)\rceil
and
\Delta=2^{i}

1

let
v\in Y
with minimal
\mu\left(B_{G[Y]}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(2k\cdot L\cdot h)}}(v,\frac{1}{4}\Delta)\right)

2

if

\mu\left(B_{G[Y]}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(2k\cdot L\cdot h)}}(v,\frac{1}{4}\Delta)\right)>\frac{1}{2}\cdot\mu_{M}(Y)

then

3

return

(Y,Y,Y)

//
Note that for every
u,v\in Y
,
d_{H}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(4k\cdot L\cdot h)}}(u,v)\leq\frac{\Delta}{2}

denote
A_{a,j}=B_{G[Y]}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}((2k\cdot a+j)\cdot h)}}\left(v,(a+\frac{j}{2k})\cdot\frac{\Delta}{4L}\right)

4

let
a\in[0,L-1]
such that
\mu\left(A_{a,0}\right)\geq\frac{\mu\left(A_{a+1,0}\right)^{2}}{\mu(Y)}

5

let
j\in[0,2(k-1)]
such that
\mu\left(A_{a,j+2}\right)\leq\mu\left(A_{a,j}\right)\cdot\left(\frac{\mu\left(A_{a+1,0}\right)}{\mu\left(A_{a,0}\right)}\right)^{\frac{1}{k}}

6

return

(A_{a,j},A_{a,j+1},A_{a,j+2})

7

Algorithm 8

(\underline{A},A,\overline{A})=\texttt{Clan-create-cluster-alt}(G[Y],\mu,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h},k,2^{i})

The proofs of the following two claims are identical to these of
3
and
4
, so we will skip them.

Claim 7
.

In
algorithm

8
of
Algorithm

8
, there is an index
a\in[0,L-1]
such that
\mu\left(A_{a,0}\right)\geq\frac{\mu\left(A_{a+1,0}\right)^{2}}{\mu(H)}
.

Claim 8
.

In
algorithm

8
of
Algorithm

8
, there is an index
j\in[0,2(k-1)]
such that
\mu_{M}\left(A_{a,j+2}\right)\leq\mu_{M}\left(A_{a,j}\right)\cdot\left(\frac{\mu_{M}\left(A_{a+1,0}\right)}{\mu_{M}\left(A_{a,0}\right)}\right)^{\frac{1}{k}}
.

The following observation is analogues to
2
, however the proof is is somewhat simpler (as there is no set
M
from which we have to choose the center, and we take into account the measure of all vertices).

Observation 5
.

Every cluster
i
-level cluster
X
(i.e. cluster on which we called
Hop-constrained-clan-embedding
with scale
2^{i}
) has diameter at most
\mathrm{diam}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(4k\cdot L\cdot h)}}(G[X])\leq 2^{i}
.

The case where the cluster is returned in
algorithm

8
is identical (to the proof of
2
). For the case where we returned the cluster in
algorithm

8
it holds that for every
v\in Y
,
\mu\left(B_{G[Y]}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(2k\cdot L\cdot h)}}(v,\frac{1}{4}\Delta)\right)>\frac{1}{2}\cdot\mu_{M}(Y)
(as
v
is a minimizes of the measure). Hence for every two points
x,y\in Y
, the balls
B_{G[Y]}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(2k\cdot L\cdot h)}}(x,\frac{1}{4}\Delta),B_{G[Y]}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(2k\cdot L\cdot h)}}(y,\frac{1}{4}\Delta)
intersect and hence
d_{G[Y]}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(4k\cdot L\cdot h)}}(x,y)\leq\frac{\Delta}{2}=2^{i}
. In particular,
\mathrm{diam}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(4k\cdot L\cdot h)}}(G[X])\leq 2^{i}
.

The proof of the following lemma follows the same lines as
Lemma

8
, and we will skip it.

Lemma 12
.

The clan embedding
(f,\chi)
has hop-distortion

a

(O(k\cdot\log\log\mu(V)),{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}O(k\cdot\log\log\mu(V))},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h})
.

Our next goal is to prove the path distortion guarantee (analogous to
Lemma

9
).
We observe the following:

Observation 6
.

Consider a cluster
\overline{A}
returned by the call
Clan-create-cluster-alt
(G[Y],\mu,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h},k,2^{i})
. Then either
\overline{A}=\underline{A}=Y
, or
\mu(\overline{A})\leq\frac{1}{2}\mu(Y)
.

Proof.

Consider the execution of the
Clan-create-cluster-alt
procedure on input
(G[Y],\mu,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h},k,2^{i})
.
If the algorithm halts in
algorithm

8
, then
\overline{A}=\underline{A}=Y
and we are done.
Otherwise, we have that
\mu\left(B_{H}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(2k\cdot L\cdot h)}}(v,\frac{1}{4}\Delta)\right)\leq\frac{1}{2}\cdot\mu_{M}(Y)
, while
\overline{A}=A_{a,j+2}
for some
a\leq L-1
and
j\leq 2(k-1)
. Hence
A_{a,j+2}\subseteq A_{L-1,2k}=B_{H}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}((2k\cdot(L-1)+2k)\cdot h)}}\left(v,(L-1+\frac{2k}{2k})\cdot\frac{\Delta}{4L}\right)=B_{H}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(2k\cdot L\cdot h)}}\left(v,\frac{\Delta}{4}\right)
, implying that
\mu(\overline{A})\leq\frac{1}{2}\cdot\mu_{M}(Y)
, as required.
∎

Given
6
, the proof of the following theorem follow the exact same lines as
Lemma

9
(replacing
O(k)
with
O(k\cdot\log\log\mu(X))
), and we will skip it (note that
Remark

1
will also hold).

Lemma 13
.

The one-to-many embedding
f
has hop path distortion
\left({O(k\cdot\log\mu(V)\cdot\log\log\mu(V))},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}\right)
.

Finally, we turn to bound
\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\mu}[|f(x)|]
, which is the main difference between the two algorithms.
Recall that for a cluster
\underline{X}
, we denote by
H(X)
the graph at the time of the creation of
\overline{X},X,\overline{X}
.

Lemma 14
.

It holds that
\mathbb{E}_{v\sim V}[|f(v)|]\leq\mu(V)^{1+\frac{1}{k}}
.

Proof.

We prove the lemma by induction on
i
and
|X|
.
Specifically, the induction hypothesis is that for an
i
-level cluster
X
, it holds that
\mathbb{E}_{x\sim X}[|f(x)|]\leq\mu(X)^{1+\frac{1}{k}}
.
Consider first the base case
where the cluster
X
created with scale
i=0
, or when

X
is a singleton
\{v\}
. The embedding will be into a single
vertex. In particular,
\mathbb{E}_{x\sim X}[|f(x)|]=\mu(\{v\})\leq\mu\left(X\right)^{1+\frac{1}{k}}
.

Next we assume that the claim holds for every
i-1
-level cluster.
Consider an
i
-level cluster
X
.
To cover
X
be called

\texttt{clan-cover}(G[X],\mu,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h},k,2^{i})
, and obtained clusters

\overline{X}_{1},\dots,\overline{X}_{s}
. Then for each cluster
\overline{X}_{q}
the algorithm created a one-to-many embedding
f_{q}
. If
s=1
, then there is only a single cluster and the lemma will follow by the induction hypothesis on
i-1
.
Else, we obtain a non-trivial cover.
Recall that after creating
\overline{X}_{1}
, the set of remaining vertices is denoted
Y_{1}=X\backslash\underline{X}_{1}
.
Note that if we were to call
clan-cover
on input
(G[Y_{1}],\mu,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h},k,2^{i})
, the algorithm will first create a cover
\overline{X}_{2},\dots,\overline{X}_{s}
of
Y_{1}
, and then will recursively create one-to-many embeddings for each
\overline{X}_{q}
.
Denote by
\tilde{f}
the one-to-many embedding created by such a call. It holds that
f=f_{1}\cup\tilde{f}
.
Therefore, we can analyze the rest of the process as if the algorithm did a recursive call on
Y_{1}
rather than on each cluster
\overline{X}_{q}
.
Since
|\overline{X}_{1}|,|Y_{1}|<|X|
, the induction hypothesis implies that
\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\overline{X}_{1}}[|f_{1}(x)|]\leq\mu(\overline{X}_{1})^{1+\frac{1}{k}}
and
\mathbb{E}_{x\sim Y_{1}}[|\tilde{f}(x)|]\leq\mu(Y_{1})^{1+\frac{1}{k}}
.
As the
Padded-partition
algorithm did not returned a trivial partition of
X
, it defined
A_{a,j}=B_{H}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}((2k\cdot a+j)\cdot h)}}\left(v,(a+\frac{j}{2k})\cdot\frac{\Delta}{4L}\right)
,
and returned
(\underline{X}_{1},X_{1},\overline{X}_{1})=(A_{a,j},A_{a,j+1},A_{a,j+2})
. The indices
a,j
where chosen such that
\mu\left(A_{a,0}\right)\geq\frac{\mu\left(A_{a+1,0}\right)^{2}}{\mu(X)}
and
\mu\left(A_{a,j+2}\right)\leq\mu\left(A_{a,j}\right)\cdot\left(\frac{\mu\left(A_{a+1,0}\right)}{\mu\left(A_{a,0}\right)}\right)^{\frac{1}{k}}
, implying

\mu\left(\overline{X}_{1}\right)\leq\mu\left(\underline{X}_{1}\right)\cdot\left(\frac{\mu\left(A_{a+1,0}\right)}{\mu\left(A_{a,0}\right)}\right)^{\frac{1}{k}}\leq\mu\left(\underline{X}_{1}\right)\cdot\left(\frac{\mu\left(X\right)}{\mu\left(A_{a+1,0}\right)}\right)^{\frac{1}{k}}\penalty\ .

It follows that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\overline{X}_{1}}[|f_{1}(x)|]

\displaystyle\leq\mu(\overline{X}_{1})^{1+\frac{1}{k}}=\mu(\overline{X}_{1})\cdot\mu(\overline{X}_{1})^{\frac{1}{k}}

\displaystyle\leq\mu\left(\underline{X}_{1}\right)\cdot\left(\frac{\mu\left(X\right)}{\mu\left(A_{a+1,0}\right)}\right)^{\frac{1}{k}}\cdot\mu(\overline{X}_{1})^{\frac{1}{k}}\leq\mu\left(\underline{X}_{1}\right)\cdot\mu\left(X\right)^{\frac{1}{k}}\penalty\ ,

where the last inequality follows as
A_{a+1,0}\supseteq A_{a,j+2}=\overline{X}_{1}
. As
X=\underline{X}_{1}\cupdot Y_{1}
, we conclude

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{x\sim X}[|f(x)|]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\overline{X}_{1}}[|f_{1}(x)|]+\mathbb{E}_{x\sim Y_{1}}[|\tilde{f}(x)|]\leq\mu\left(\underline{X}_{1}\right)\cdot\mu\left(X\right)^{\frac{1}{k}}+\mu\left(Y_{1}\right)\cdot\mu\left(X\right)^{\frac{1}{k}}=\mu\left(X\right)^{1+\frac{1}{k}}\penalty\ .

∎

Lemma

11
now follows by combining
Lemma

12
,
Lemma

13
, and
Lemma

14
.

An observation that will be useful in some of our applications (specifically for
Theorem

11
and
Corollary

2
) is the following:

Observation 7
.

Suppose that
Lemma

11
is applied on a measure
\mu
where for some vertex
r\in V
,
\mu(r)>\frac{1}{2}\mu(V)
. Then in the resulting clan embedding
(f,\chi)
it will hold that
|f(r)|=1
.

Proof.

Consider an execution of the
Clan-create-cluster-alt
(G[Y],\mu,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h},k,2^{i})
(
Algorithm

8
) procedure where
r\in Y
.
The algorithm picks a center
v
that minimizes
\mu\left(B_{Y}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(2k\cdot L\cdot h)}}(v,\frac{1}{4}\Delta)\right)
. Note that every ball containing
r
have measure strictly larger then
\frac{1}{2}\mu(V)
, while every ball that does not contain
r
has measure strictly less than
\frac{1}{2}\mu(V)
.
Thus by
6
, either
\underline{A}=Y
, or
\mu(\overline{A})\leq\frac{1}{2}\mu(Y)
, which implies
r\notin\overline{A}
.
If follows that in any call to the
clan-cover
procedure that returns
(\overline{X}_{1},\dots,\overline{X}_{s})
,
r
will belong only to
\overline{X}_{s}
. The observation now follows by induction.
∎

6
Subgraph preserving one-to-many embedding

In our clan embeddings (
Theorems

3
and
4
) we had the hop-path-distortion property, which ensures that every path
P
in
G
has a “corresponding” copy
P_{T}
in the tree
T
of similar weight. However, as
T
contains many vertices which are not copies of
G
vertices, it is not immediately clear to which subgraph of
G
,
P_{T}
corresponds. To clarify this point, we introduce the notion of
Path Tree One-To-Many Embedding
(
Definition

8
). Here all
T
vertices are copies of
V
, end every edge
e\in T
corresponds to a path
P^{T}_{e}
between its endpoints. Now, there is a natural way to translate from subgraphs of
T
back to subgraphs of
G
. Morally,
Corollaries

1
and
2
below are just a translation of
Lemmas

7
and
11
(respectively) to the language of path tree one-to-many embeddings.

A major drawback of
Corollaries

1
and
2
is that it only applies on
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}
-respecting subgraphs.
Specifically, in order to apply the “subgraph preservation” guarantee, the subgraph
H
in question must be “local” (formally it is not allowed to contain a
u-v
path of weight less than
d_{G}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(h)}}(u,v)
).
This is problematic, as in some of our applications the solution subgraph is not
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}
-respecting (e.g. in h.c. group Steiner forest).
From the other hand, clearly one cannot simply drop the
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}
-respecting condition from
Corollaries

1
and
2
(or
Theorems

3
and
4
).
Indeed, consider the
n
-path (a graph consisting only of the edges of the length-
n
path:
(v_{0},\dots,v_{n-1})
) with hop parameter
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}=1
, and let
H=G
. The distance between every pair of copies
v^{\prime}_{0}\in f(v_{0}),v^{\prime}_{n-1}\in f(v_{n-1})
has to be
\infty
. Thus any “image” of the entire graph has to be of weight
\infty
.

In
Theorems

5
and
11
we give a weaker guarantee which can be applied on arbitrary subgraphs. Specifically, given a subgraph
H
of
G
, we construct a (not necessarily connected) subgraph
H^{\prime}
of
T
where for every
u,v\in H
such that
\mathrm{hop}_{H}(u,v)\leq{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}
,
H^{\prime}
contains a pair of copies
u^{\prime}\in f(u)
and
v^{\prime}\in f(v)
in the same connected component. Further,
w_{T}(H^{\prime})\leq O(k\cdot\log n)\cdot w(H)
.
This guarantee will be sufficient for our applications.

This section is devoted to proving
Theorems

5
and
11
, which insures that any arbitrary subgraph
H
of
G
will have a “corresponding” copy in the image.
We begin the section by formally defining Path Tree One-To-Many Embedding (very similar definition was defined by
[
HHZ21b
]
). See
Figure

5
for illustration.

Definition 8

(Path Tree One-To-Many Embedding)
.

A one-to-many path tree embedding of a weighted graph
G=(V,E,w_{G})
consists of a dominating one-to-many embedding
f
into a tree
T
such that
f(V)=V(T)
, and associated paths
\{P^{T}_{e}\}_{e\in E(T)}
, where for every edge
e=\{u^{\prime},v^{\prime}\}\in E(T)
where
u^{\prime}\in f(u)
and
v^{\prime}\in f(v)
,
P^{T}_{e}
is a path from
u
to
v
in
G
of weight at most
w_{T}(\{u^{\prime},v^{\prime}\})
.

For every two vertices
u^{\prime},v^{\prime}\in V(T)
, let
e_{1},e_{2},\dots,e_{s}
be the unique simple path between them in
T
. We denote:
P^{T}_{u^{\prime}v^{\prime}}=P^{T}_{e_{1}}\circ P^{T}_{e_{2}}\circ\cdots\circ P^{T}_{e_{s}}
to be the concatenation of all the associated paths (note that
P^{T}_{u^{\prime}v^{\prime}}
is not necessarily simple).
We say that
f
has hop-bound
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\beta}
if for every
u^{\prime},v^{\prime}\in V(T)
which are connected in
T
,
\mathrm{hop}(P^{T}_{u^{\prime}v^{\prime}})\leq{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\beta}
.

Figure 5:
The graph
G
is illustrated on the left, while the path tree one-to-many embedding into the tree
T
is illustrated on the right.
Each edge
e\in T
has an associated path
P^{T}_{e}
, for example
P^{T}_{e^{\prime},d}=(e,d)
, and
P^{T}_{a^{\prime},e}=(a,f,e)
. Each pair of vertices
u,v\in V(T)
, has an (not necessarily simple) induced path
P^{T}_{u,v}
, for example
P^{T}_{f,e^{\prime}}=(f,b,c,d,e)
, and
P^{T}_{a^{\prime},e^{\prime}}=(a,f,e,b,c,d,e)
. The hop-bound
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\beta}
is the maximal number of hops in an inducted path. Here
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\beta}=6
(realized by
P_{T}^{a^{\prime},e^{\prime}}
).

We begin by translating
Lemma

7
to the language of path tree embeddings.

Lemma 15
.

Given an
n
-point weighted graph
G=(V,E,w_{G})
with polynomial aspect ratio, a vertex
r\in V
,
and integers
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h},k\geq 1
, there is a dominating clan embedding
(f,\chi)
into a
tree
T
such that:

1.

|f(V)|\leq(2n)^{1+\frac{1}{k}}
and
|f(r)|=1
.

2.

(f,\chi)
has hop-distortion
\left(O(k),{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}O(k\cdot\log n)},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}\right)
, and hop-path-distortion
\left({O(k\cdot\log n)},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}\right)
.

3.

f
is a path tree one-to-many embedding with associated paths
\{P^{T}_{e}\}_{e\in E(T)}
and hop-bound
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}O(k\cdot\log^{2}n)\cdot h}
.

Proof.

First apply
Lemma

7
on the graph
G=(V,E,w)

with parameter
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}
,
k
, and
(\geq 1)
-measure
\mu
where
\mu(r)=n
, and for every
v\neq r
,
\mu(v)=1
.
As a result we will obtain a dominating clan embedding
(f_{U},\chi_{U})
into an ultrametric
U
, where
(1)
|f_{U}(V)|\leq\mu(V)^{1+\frac{1}{k}}=(2n)^{1+\frac{1}{k}}
,
(2)
\forall u,v\in V
,
d_{G}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(O(k\cdot\log n)\cdot h)}}(u,v)\leq\min_{v^{\prime}\in f_{U}(v),u^{\prime}\in f_{U}(u)}d_{U}(v^{\prime},u^{\prime})
,
(3)
\forall u,v\in V
,
\min_{v^{\prime}\in f_{U}(v)}d_{U}(v^{\prime},\chi(u))\leq O(k)\cdot d_{G}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(h)}}(u,v)
,
(4)
f
has hop-path distortion
\left(O(k\cdot\log n),{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}\right)
,
and finally, (5) as
\mu(r)>\frac{1}{2}\mu(V)
,
4
implies that
|f_{U}(r)|=1
.

Next, we transform the ultrametric into a tree.
Gupta
[
Gup01
]
,
8
8

8

See
[
FKT19
]
for an alternative proof. We refer to
[
Fil19b
,
Fil20
]
for further details on the Steiner point removal problem.

showed that given a tree
\mathcal{T}
and a subset of terminals
K\subseteq V(\mathcal{T})
, one can (efficiently) embed (in a classic one-to-one fashion) the set of terminals
K
into a tree
\mathcal{T}_{K}
such that
V(\mathcal{T}_{K})=K
, and for every
u,v\in K
,
d_{\mathcal{T}}(u,v)\leq d_{\mathcal{T}_{K}}(u,v)\leq 8\cdot d_{\mathcal{T}}(u,v)
.
That is, Gupta removed all the non-terminal (Steiner) points, and distorted all the terminal pairwise distances by a multiplicative factor of at most
8
.
Applying
[
Gup01
]
on the ultrametric
U
with
f_{U}(V)
as the set of terminals
K
, and concatenating the two embeddings, we obtain a clan embedding
(f,\chi)
into a tree
\mathcal{T}_{K}
over
f_{U}(V)
such that
\forall u,v\in V
,

\displaystyle d_{G}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(O(k\cdot\log n)\cdot h)}}(u,v)

\displaystyle\leq\min_{v^{\prime}\in f(v),u^{\prime}\in f(u)}d_{\mathcal{T}_{K}}(v^{\prime},u^{\prime})

\displaystyle\min_{v^{\prime}\in f(v)}d_{\mathcal{T}_{K}}(v^{\prime},\chi(u))

\displaystyle\leq O(k)\cdot d_{G}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(h)}}(u,v)\penalty\ .

For every sequence of vertices
v^{\prime}_{0},\dots,v^{\prime}_{s}
in
f_{U}(V)
, it holds that
\sum_{i=0}^{s-1}d_{\mathcal{T}_{K}}(v^{\prime}_{i},v^{\prime}_{i+1})\leq 8\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{s-1}d_{U}(v^{\prime}_{i},v^{\prime}_{i+1})
.
As
f_{U}
has hop-path distortion
\left(O(k\cdot\log n),{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}\right)
, if follows that
f
has hop-path distortion
\left(O(k\cdot\log n),{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}\right)
as well.

Finally, for every edge
e=\{u^{\prime},v^{\prime}\}\in E(T)
where
u^{\prime}\in f(u)
and
v^{\prime}\in f(v)
, let
P^{\mathcal{T}_{K}}_{e}
be a path of minimum weight among all the
u
-
v
paths in
G
with at most
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}O(k\cdot\log n)\cdot h}
hops. Note that
w_{G}(P_{e}^{\mathcal{T}_{K}})\leq d_{G}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(O(k\cdot\log n)\cdot h)}}(u,v)\leq d_{\mathcal{T}_{K}}(u^{\prime},v^{\prime})
.
Following
Algorithm

5
, as
G
has polynomial aspect ratio, the ultrametric
U
has depth
O(\log n)
. In particular, every path in
U
consists of at most
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}O(\log n)}
hops.
The tree
\mathcal{T}_{K}
constructed by
[
Gup01
]
(see also
[
FKT19
]
) is a minor of
U
constructed by contracting edges only. Therefore, the hop-diameter of
\mathcal{T}_{K}
is bounded by
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}O(\log n)}
as well.
Consider
u^{\prime},v^{\prime}\in f(V)
, then
P^{\mathcal{T}_{K}}_{u^{\prime}v^{\prime}}
consist of concatenating at most
O(\log n)
paths, each with
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}O(k\cdot\log n)\cdot h}
hops. It follows that
\mathrm{hop}(P^{\mathcal{T}_{K}}_{u^{\prime}v^{\prime}})\leq{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}O(k\cdot\log^{2}n)\cdot h}
as required.
∎

Recall that a subgraph
H
of
G
is
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}
-respecting if for every
u,v\in H
. It holds that
d^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(h)}}_{G}(u,v)\leq d_{H}(u,v)
.
The following is a corollary of
Lemma

15
.

Corollary 1
.

Consider an
n
-point graph
G=(V,E,w)
with polynomial aspect ratio, parameters
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h},k\in\mathbb{N}
and vertex
r\in V
.
Then there is a path-tree one-to-many embedding
f
of
G
into a tree
T
with hop bound
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}O(k\cdot\log^{2}n)\cdot h}
,
|f(V)|=(2n)^{1+\frac{1}{k}}
,
|f(r)|=1
, and such that for every connected
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}
-respecting sub-graph
H
, there is a connected subgraph
H^{\prime}
of
T
where for every
u\in H
,
H^{\prime}
contains some vertex from
f(u)
. Further,
w_{T}(H^{\prime})\leq O(k\cdot\log n)\cdot w(H)
.

Proof.

We apply
Lemma

15
on
G
. All the properties other than the last one hold immediately. Consider an
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}
-respecting subgraph
H
of
G
.
We double each edge in
H
, to obtain a multi-graph, where all the vertices have even degree. In particular, there is an Eulerian tour
P=(x_{0},\dots,x_{s})
traversing all the edges in
H
. Note that for every
a,b\in[0,s]
, as
H
is
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}
-respecting subgraph of
G
, it holds that

d_{P}(x_{a},x_{b})=d_{H}(x_{a},x_{b})\geq d^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(h)}}_{G}(x_{a},x_{b})\penalty\ .

Thus
P
is an
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}
-respecting path.
It follows that there are vertices
x^{\prime}_{0},\dots,x^{\prime}_{s}
where
x^{\prime}_{i}\in f(x_{i})
such that
\sum_{i=0}^{s-1}d_{T}(x^{\prime}_{i},x^{\prime}_{i+1})\leq O(k\cdot\log n)\cdot\sum_{i=0}^{s-1}w(v_{i},v_{i+1})=O(k\cdot\log n)\cdot 2\cdot w(H)
.
The corollary follows.
∎

Finally, we will state the result in this section in an alternative form, where instead of
Lemma

7
, we will use
Lemma

11
.
In the counter-part of
Lemma

15
we will obtain the same result with the only changes that

(f,\chi)
will have hop-distortion
\left(O(k\cdot\log\log n),{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}O(k\cdot\log\log n)},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}\right)
, hop-path-distortion
\left({O(k\cdot\log n\cdot\log\log n)},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}\right)
, and the hop bound of the path tree one-to-many embedding will be
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}O(k\cdot\log n\cdot\log\log n)\cdot h}
.
The following corollary follow the same lines:

Corollary 2
.

Consider an
n
-point graph
G=(V,E,w)
with polynomial aspect ratio, parameters
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h},k\in\mathbb{N}
and vertex
r\in V
.
Then there is a path-tree one-to-many embedding
f
of
G
into a tree
T
with hop bound
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}O(k\cdot\log n\cdot\log\log n)\cdot h}
,
|f(V)|=(2n)^{1+\frac{1}{k}}
,
|f(r)|=1
, and such that for every connected
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}
-respecting sub-graph
H
, there is a connected subgraph
H^{\prime}
of
T
where for every
u\in H
,
H^{\prime}
contains some vertex from
f(u)
. Further,
w_{T}(H^{\prime})\leq O(k\cdot\log n\cdot\log\log n)\cdot w(H)
.

6.1
General subgraph: Proofs of
Theorems

5
and
11

Both
Corollary

1
and
Corollary

2
have a major drawback: the guarantee is only w.r.t.
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}
-respecting sub-graphs.
Unfortunately, the
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}
-respecting condition is indeed necessary (as the image tree
T
should not allow as to connect vertices at large hop-distance).
Here we ensure the next best thing: given an arbitrary subgraph
H
of
G
, we will construct a (not necessarily connected) subgraph
H^{\prime}
of
T
where for every
u,v\in H
such that
\mathrm{hop}_{H}(u,v)\leq{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}
,
H^{\prime}
contains a pair of copies
u^{\prime}\in f(u)
and
v^{\prime}\in f(v)
in the same connected component. Further,
w_{T}(H^{\prime})\leq O(k\cdot\log n)\cdot w(H)
.
As a result, the hop bound will increase by a
\log
factor.

See
5

Proof.

Set
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h^{\prime}}={\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h\cdot 4\log n}
. We execute
Corollary

1
with hop parameter
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h^{\prime}}
. As a result we obtain a path-tree one-to-many embedding
f
into a tree
T
with hop bound
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}O(k\cdot\log^{2}n)\cdot h^{\prime}}={\color[rgb]{1,0,0}O(k\cdot\log^{3}n)\cdot h}
,
|f(V)|=O(2n)^{1+\frac{1}{k}}
, and
|f(r)|=1
. It only remains to prove the subgraph preservation property, which will be based on the following lemma.

Lemma 16

(Sparse Cover)
.

Consider an
n
-vertex weighted graph
G=(V,E,w)
, with cost function
\mu:E\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, and parameter
\Delta>0
. Then there is a collection of clusters
C_{1},C_{2},\dots,C_{s}
such that:

1.

Each
C_{i}
has radius at most
\Delta\cdot\log(2n)
. That is, there is a vertex
v_{i}\in C_{i}
such that
\max_{u\in C_{i}}d_{G[C_{i}]}(u,v_{i})\leq\Delta\cdot\log(2n)
.

2.

Every pair of vertices
u,v
such that
d_{G}(u,v)\leq\Delta
belongs to some
C_{i}
.

3.

\sum_{i=1}^{s}\mu(C_{i})\leq 4\cdot\mu(G)
, where
\mu(C)=\sum_{e\subseteq C}\mu(e)
.

We will now proceed with the proof of
Theorem

5
. The proof (and some historical context) of
Lemma

16
is deferred to
Section

6.2
.
Consider a subgraph
H=(V_{H},E_{H},w)
of
G
. Let
H_{1}=(V_{H},E_{H})
be the graph
H
where all the edge weights are changed to
1
. Set
\Delta={\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}
, and a cost function
\mu:E_{H}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, where
\mu(e)=w(e)
.
Using
Lemma

16
, we obtain a collection of clusters
C_{1},\dots,C_{s}\subseteq V_{H}
. Let
F_{i}
be the shortest path tree rooted in
v_{i}
spanning all the vertices in
C_{i}
w.r.t. distances in
H_{1}
.
Note that as
H_{1}
is unweighted, by
property (
1
)
of
Lemma

16
,
F_{i}
is a tree of depth at most
\Delta\cdot\log 2n
. In particular, every path in
F_{i}
contains at most
2\cdot\Delta\cdot\log 2n\leq{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}\cdot 4\log n={\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h^{\prime}}
hops. It follows that
F_{i}
(when considered as a subgraph of
G
with the original weight function
w
) is
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h^{\prime}}
-respecting subgraph of
G
(this is as every
u
-
v
path in
F_{i}
consist of at most
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h^{\prime}}
hops, an thus have weight at least
d_{G}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(h^{\prime})}}(u,v)
).

By
Corollary

1
, there is a connected subgraph
T_{i}
of
T
where for every
u\in F_{i}
,
T_{i}
contains some vertex from
f(u)
. Further,
w_{T}(T_{i})\leq O(k\cdot\log n)\cdot w(F_{i})
. Set
H^{\prime}=\cup_{i=1}^{s}T_{i}
.
Note that for every
u,v\in H
such that
\mathrm{hop}_{H}(u,v)\leq{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}
, it holds that
d_{H_{1}}(u,v)\leq{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}
, and hence by
Lemma

16
there is some cluster
C_{i}
containing both
u,v
. According to
Corollary

1
,
T_{i}\subseteq H^{\prime}
is a connected subgraph containing vertices from both
f(u)
and
f(v)
, as required. Finally, we bound the weight of
H^{\prime}
:

\displaystyle w_{T}(H^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{s}w_{T}(T_{i})=O(k\cdot\log n)\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{s}w(F_{i})\leq O(k\cdot\log n)\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{s}w(C_{i})

\displaystyle=O(k\cdot\log n)\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{s}\mu(C_{i})=O(k\cdot\log n)\cdot\mu(H)=O(k\cdot\log n)\cdot w(H)\penalty\ .

∎

By using
Corollary

2
instead of
Corollary

1
in the proof above, we obtain the following:

Theorem 11
.

Consider an
n
-point graph
G=(V,E,w)
with polynomial aspect ratio, parameters
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h},k\in\mathbb{N}
and vertex
r\in V
.
Then there is a path-tree one-to-many embedding
f
of
G
into a tree
T
with hop bound
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}O(k\cdot\log^{2}n\cdot\log\log n)\cdot h}
,
|f(V)|=(2n)^{1+\frac{1}{k}}
,
|f(r)|=1
, and such that for every sub-graph
H
, there is a subgraph
H^{\prime}
of
T
where for every
u,v\in H
with
\mathrm{hop}_{H}(u,v)\leq{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}
,
H^{\prime}
contains a pair of copies
u^{\prime}\in f(u)
and
v^{\prime}\in f(v)
in the same connected component. Further,
w_{T}(H^{\prime})\leq O(k\cdot\log n\cdot\log\log n)\cdot w(H)
.

6.2
Proof of
Lemma

16
: sparse cover

Sparse covers were first introduced by Awerbuch and Peleg
[
AP90
]
, and were studied in various graph families (see e.g.
[
KPR93
,
Fil19a
,
Fil20
,
Fil24
]
). We refer to
[
Fil19a
,
Fil20
]
for farther details.
For an integer parameter
k\geq 1
, and
\Delta>0
, Awerbuch and Peleg partitioned a general
n
-vertex graph into clusters of radius at most
(2k-1)\cdot\Delta
such that every ball of radius
\Delta
is fully contained in some cluster, and every vertex belongs to at most
2k\cdot n^{\frac{1}{k}}
different clusters. In particular, by fixing
k=\log n
, one will get all the properties in
Lemma

16
, but the bound on
\sum_{i}\mu(C_{i})
will only be
4\log n\cdot\mu(G)
(we aim for
4\cdot\mu(G)
).
While the construction in
[
AP90
]
is deterministic, here we will present a random ball growing clustering algorithm (ala
[
Bar96
]
). As a result, while in the worst case a vertex might belong to
\log n
different clusters, in expectation, each vertex will belong only to a constant number. A bound on the total weight will follow.

Proof of
Lemma

16
.

By scaling, we can assume that
\Delta=1
. Let
\mathsf{Geo}(\frac{1}{2})
be geometric distribution with parameter
\frac{1}{2}
(that is getting value
i\geq 1
with probability
2^{-i}
).
Each created cluster
C
will have an interior part denoted
\mathsf{int}(C)
, that will be contained in
C
.
We create a collection of clusters
\mathcal{C}
as follows, initially
Y_{1}=V
is all the unclustered vertices. At step
i
, after we created the clusters
C_{1},C_{2},\dots,C_{i-1}
, the unclustered vertices are
Y_{i}=V\setminus(\cup_{j<i}\mathsf{int}(C_{j}))
. Pick an arbitrary vertex
x_{i}\in Y_{i}
, and a radius
r_{i}\sim\mathsf{Geo}(\frac{1}{2})
. Set the new cluster to be
C_{i}=B_{G[Y_{i}]}(x_{i},r_{i})
the ball of radius
r_{i}
around
x_{i}
in the graph induced by the unclustered vertices, and its interior to
\mathsf{int}(C_{i})=B_{G[Y_{i}]}(x_{i},r_{i}-1)
. The process halts once
Y_{i}=\emptyset
.
See illustration on the right.

Consider a pair
u,v
such that
d_{G}(u,v)\leq 1
, and let
P
be the shortest path between them. Let
i
be the first index such that
\mathsf{int}(C_{i})\cap P\neq\emptyset
, and let
z\in\mathsf{int}(C_{i})\cap P
. By the minimality of
i
,
P\subseteq Y_{i}
.
As
z\in\mathsf{int}(C_{i})
it follows that
d_{G[Y_{i}]}(x_{i},z)\leq r_{i}-1
. By triangle inequality
d_{G[Y_{i}]}(x_{i},u)\leq d_{G[Y_{i}]}(x_{i},z)+d_{G[Y_{i}]}(z,u)\leq r_{i}
, implying that
u\in C_{i}
. Similarly,
v\in C_{i}
.
Property (
2
)
of
Lemma

16
follows.

The radius of the cluster
C_{i}
is bounded by
r_{i}
. Denoted by
\Psi
the event that
r_{i}\leq\log(2n)
for every
i
.
By union bound, as in the entire process we create at most
n
balls,

\Pr\left[\overline{\Psi}\right]=\Pr\left[\exists i\text{ s.t. }r_{i}>\log(2n)\right]\leq n\cdot 2^{-\log(2n)}=\frac{1}{2}\penalty\ .

Denote by
X_{v}
the number of cluster containing a vertex
v
. Note that given that
v
belongs to a cluster
C_{i}
, by the memoryless property of geometric distribution, the probability that
v
belongs to the the interior of the cluster is
\frac{1}{2}
:

\Pr\left[v\in\mathsf{int}(C_{i})\mid v\in C_{i}\right]=\Pr\left[d_{G}(x_{i},v)\leq r_{i}-1\mid d_{G}(x_{i},v)\leq r_{i}\right]=\frac{1}{2}\penalty\ .

Once
v
belongs to the interior of a cluster
\mathsf{int}(C_{i})
, it will not belong to any other clusters. Hence
X_{v}
is dominated by a geometric distribution with parameter
\frac{1}{2}
. In particular,
\mathbb{E}[X_{v}]\leq 2
.
For an edge
e=\{u,v\}
denote by
X_{e}
the number of clusters containing
e
. Then clearly
\mathbb{E}[X_{e}]\leq\mathbb{E}[X_{v}]\leq 2
. We conclude:

\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{i\geq 1}\mu(C_{i})\right]=\sum_{e\in E}\mu(e)\cdot\mathbb{E}\left[X_{e}\right]\leq 2\cdot\mu(G)\penalty\ .

We condition the execution of the algorithm on the event
\Psi
. Then clearly
property (
1
)
holds. Using the law of total probability, the expected cost is bounded by

\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{i\geq 1}\mu(C_{i})\mid\Psi\right]=\frac{1}{\Pr\left[\Psi\right]}\cdot\left(\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{i\geq 1}\mu(C_{i})\right]-\Pr\left[\overline{\Psi}\right]\cdot\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{i\geq 1}\mu(C_{i})\mid\overline{\Psi}\right]\right)\leq\nicefrac{{2}}{{\frac{1}{2}}}=4\penalty\ .

Property (
3
)
now follows as well.

.
∎

7
Approximation algorithms

7.1
Application of the Ramsey type embedding

Our
Theorems

1
and
2
is similar in spirit to a theorem in Haeupler
et al.
[
HHZ21b
]
:

Theorem 12

(
[
HHZ21b
]
)
.

Consider an
n
-vertex graph
G=(V,E,w)
, and parameters
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}\in[n]
,
\epsilon\in(0,\frac{1}{3})
. There is distribution
\mathcal{D}
over pairs
(M,U)
where
M\subseteq V
, and
U
is an ultrametric over
M
such that:

1.

For every
v\in V
,
\Pr[v\in M]\geq 1-\epsilon
.

2.

For every
v,u\in M
,
d_{G}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(O(\log^{2}n)\cdot h)}}(u,v)\leq d_{U}(u,v)\leq O(\frac{\log^{2}n}{\epsilon})\cdot d_{G}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(h)}}(u,v)
.

Note that our
Theorem

1
implies
[
HHZ21b
]

Theorem

12
with an improved distortion parameter of
O(\frac{\log n}{\epsilon})
, while our
Theorem

2
implies
[
HHZ21b
]

Theorem

12
with an improved hop-stretch guarantee of
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}O(\frac{\log n\cdot\log\log n}{\epsilon})}
and improved distortion parameter of
O(\frac{\log n\cdot\log\log n}{\epsilon})
.

[
HHZ21b
]
applied
Theorem

12
to obtain many different approximation algorithms.
While their application is not straightforward,
one can use
Theorem

1
or
Theorem

2
instead of
Theorem

12
, to obtain improved approximation factors in all the approximation algorithms in
[
HHZ21b
]
. We refer to
[
HHZ21b
]
for problem definitions, background, approximation algorithms and their analysis, and only summarize the results in the following corollary. See
Table

1
for comparison between ours to previous results.

Corollary 3
.

The hop-constrained version of each of the following problems has poly-time approximation algorithm as follows:

1.

The oblivious Steiner forest with
O(\log^{2}n)
-cost approximation and
O(\log^{3}n)
hop approximation.
Alternatively, one can obtain
\tilde{O}(\log^{2}n)
-cost approximation and
\tilde{O}(\log^{2}n)
hop approximation.

2.

The relaxed k-Steiner tree with
O(\log n)
-cost approximation and
O(\log^{3}n)
hop approximation.
Alternatively, one can obtain
\tilde{O}(\log n)
-cost approximation and
\tilde{O}(\log^{2}n)
hop approximation.

3.

The k-Steiner tree with
O(\log n\cdot\log k)
-cost approximation and
O(\log^{3}n)
hop approximation.
Alternatively, one can obtain
\tilde{O}(\log n\cdot\log k)
-cost approximation and
\tilde{O}(\log^{2}n)
hop approximation.

4.

The oblivious network design with
O(\log^{3}n)
-cost approximation and
O(\log^{3}n)
hop approximation. Alternatively, one can obtain
\tilde{O}(\log^{3}n)
-cost approximation and
\tilde{O}(\log^{2}n)
hop approximation.

5.

The group Steiner tree with
O(\log^{3}n\cdot\log k)
-cost approximation and
O(\log^{3}n)
hop approximation. Alternatively, one can obtain
\tilde{O}(\log^{3}n\cdot\log k)
-cost approximation and
\tilde{O}(\log^{2}n)
hop approximation.

6.

The online group Steiner tree with
O(\log^{4}n\cdot\log k)
-cost approximation and
O(\log^{3}n)
hop approximation. Alternatively, one can obtain
\tilde{O}(\log^{4}n\cdot\log k)
-cost approximation and
\tilde{O}(\log^{2}n)
hop approximation.

7.

The group Steiner forest with
O(\log^{5}n\cdot\log k)
-cost approximation and
O(\log^{3}n)
hop approximation. Alternatively, one can obtain
\tilde{O}(\log^{5}n\cdot\log k)
-cost approximation and
\tilde{O}(\log^{2}n)
hop approximation.

8.

The online group Steiner forest with
O(\log^{6}n\cdot\log k)
-cost approximation and
O(\log^{3}n)
hop approximation. Alternatively, one can obtain
\tilde{O}(\log^{6}n\cdot\log k)
-cost approximation and
\tilde{O}(\log^{2}n)
hop approximation.

Most notably, the state of the art bicriteria approximation for the h.c.
k
-Steiner tree problem
5
was by Khani and Salavatipour
[
KS16
]
who obtain
O(\log^{2}n)
cost approximation and
O(\log n)
hop-stretch (improving over
[
HKS09
]
).
Note that our
O(\log n\cdot\log k)
cost approximation is strictly superior, while our hop-stretch is inferior.

7.2
Application of the clan embedding

For the h.c. relaxed k-Steiner tree, h.c. k-Steiner tree, h.c. oblivious Steiner forest, and h.c. oblivious network design problems
Corollary

3
is the best approximation we obtain (by simply replacing
Theorem

12
with
Theorems

1
and
2
).
For the h.c. group Steiner tree/forest problems, and their online versions, we provide better approximation factors by going through clan embeddings, and in particular the subgraph preservation properties of
Corollaries

1
and
2
and
Theorems

5
and
11
.

[
HHZ21b
]
had an analogous theorem to
Theorem

5
, while the cost was

O(\log^{3}n)
instead of our
O(\log n)
(see Theorem 8 in their
arXiv version
). Using our
Theorems

5
and
11
provides a
O(\log^{2}n)
multiplicative improvement for the h.c. group Steiner forest problem and its online version. To illustrate the strength of
Theorems

5
and
11
, we provide full details in
Sections

7.2.3
and
7.2.4
.
Further, for the h.c. group Steiner tree problem and its online version the optimal solution is an
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}
-respecting subgraph, and hence we can use
Corollaries

1
and
2
instead of
Theorems

5
and
11
, and thus shaving an
O(\log n)
factor from the hop stretch, see
Sections

7.2.1
and
7.2.2
for details.
See
Table

1
for comparison between ours to previous results.

7.2.1
Hop-constrained group Steiner tree

In the group Steiner tree problem we are given
k
sets
g_{1},\dots,g_{k}\subseteq V
, and a root vertex
r\in V
. The goal is to construct a minimal weight tree spanning the vertex
r
and at least one vertex from each group (set)
g_{i}
.
In general, the state of the art is an
O(\log^{2}n\cdot\log k)
-approximation
[
GKR00
]
.
In the h.c. version of the problem, we are given in addition a hop-bound
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}
, and the requirement is to find a minimum weight subgraph
H
, such that for every
g_{i}
,
\mathrm{hop}_{H}(r,g_{i})\leq{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}
.
Denote by
\mathrm{OPT}
the weight of the optimal solution.

[
HHZ21b
]
provided a bicriteria approximation by constructing a subgraph
H
of weight
O(\log^{4}n\cdot\log k)\cdot\mathrm{OPT}
, such that for every
i
,
\mathrm{hop}_{H}(r,g_{i})\leq{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}O(\log^{3}n)\cdot h}
.
We restate our improvement:

See
6

Note that ignoring the hop-constrains, our approximation factor is matching the state of the art
[
GKR00
]
.

Proof of
Theorem

6
.

We apply
Corollary

1
with hop parameter
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h^{\prime}}={\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2h}
, stretch parameter
2
, and
r
as the special vertex, to obtain path-tree one-to-many embedding
f
into a tree
T
, with associated paths
\{P^{T}_{e}\}_{e\in E(T)}
. For every
i
, let
\tilde{g}_{i}=f(g_{i})
, and set
\tilde{r}
to be the only vertex in
f(r)
.
Garg, Konjevod, and Ravi
[
GKR00
]
constructed an
O(\log n\cdot\log k)
approximation algorithm for the group Steiner tree problem in trees. We apply their algorithm on the tree
T
with center
\tilde{r}
and the groups
\tilde{g}_{1},\dots,\tilde{g}_{k}
. Suppose that
[
GKR00
]
return the solution
\tilde{H}\subseteq T
. Our solution to the original problem will be
H=\bigcup_{e\in\tilde{H}}P^{T}_{e}
.
9
9

9

If it is desirable that the obtained solution will be a tree, one can simply take a shortest path tree rooted at
r
w.r.t. unweighted
H
. This will be a valid solution of at most the same weight.

For every
i
,
\tilde{H}
contains a path
P_{i}=e_{1},\dots,e_{s}
from
\tilde{r}
to some vertex
v^{\prime}\in\tilde{G}_{i}=f(g_{i})
, in particular
v^{\prime}\in f(v)
for some
v\in g_{i}
. It follows that the solution we will return
H
will contain a path
P^{T}_{\tilde{r},v^{\prime}}
between
r
to
v
. As the path-tree one-to-many embedding
f
has hop-bound
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}O(\log^{2}n)\cdot h^{\prime}}
, it follows that
\mathrm{hop}_{H}(r,g_{i})\leq{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}O(\log^{2}n)\cdot h}
as required.
We conclude that
H
is a valid solution to the problem.

Next we bound the weight of
H
.
Let
F
be an optimal solution in
G
of weight
\mathrm{OPT}
. Note that we can assume that
F
is a tree, as otherwise we can take the shortest path tree w.r.t. the unweighted version of the graph
F
rooted at
r
. The result will be still a valid solution of weight at most
\mathrm{OPT}
.
Note that for every
u\in F
,
\mathrm{hop}_{F}(r,u)\leq{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}
, as otherwise we can remove this vertex from
F
. As
F
is a tree, every pair of vertices
u,v\in F
, have a unique path between them with at most
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2h}
hops, in particular
d_{G}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(2h)}}(u,v)\leq d_{F}(u,v)
. We conclude that
F
is
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h^{\prime}}
-respecting.
Hence by
Corollary

1
,
T
contains a connected graph
\tilde{F}
, such that for every
u\in F
,
\tilde{F}
contains some vertex in
f(u)
, and
w_{T}(\tilde{F})=O(\log n)\cdot w_{G}(F)=O(\log n)\cdot\mathrm{OPT}
.
Note that
\tilde{F}
is a valid solution to the group Steiner tree problem in
T
. Hence by
[
GKR00
]
,

w_{G}(H)\leq w_{T}(\tilde{H})\leq O(\log n\cdot\log k)\cdot w_{T}(\tilde{F})=O(\log^{2}n\cdot\log k)\cdot\mathrm{OPT}\penalty\ .

as required.
The alternative tradeoff is obtained by using
Corollary

2
instead of
Corollary

1
.
∎

7.2.2
Online hop-constrained group Steiner tree

The online group Steiner tree problem is an online version of the group Steiner tree problem. Here we are given a graph
G=(V,E,w)
with a root
r\in V
. The groups
g_{1},g_{2},\dots
arrive one at a time. The goal is to maintain trees
T_{1},T_{2},\dots
such that for every
i
,
T_{i-1}\subseteq T_{i}
, and such that in
T_{i}
,
r
is connected to at least one vertex in
g_{i}
.
An algorithm has
competitive ratio

t
if for every sequence of sets
g_{1},\dots,g_{i}
, the tree
T_{i}
has weight at most
t
times larger than the optimal solution for the offline group Steiner tree problem with input groups
g_{1},\dots,g_{i}
(and root
r
).
For randomized algorithms, we say that the algorithm has competitive ratio
\alpha
, if for every input sequence (fixed in advance, unknown by the algorithm) the expected ratio between the obtained solution to the optimal one is at most
\alpha
.

As was shown by Alon
et al.
[
AAA+06
]
, in full generality, no sub-polynomial approximation is possible.
Therefore, we will assume that there is a set of subsets
\mathcal{K}\subseteq 2^{V}
of size
k
, and all the sets
g_{i}
are chosen from
\mathcal{K}
.
For this version,
[
AAA+06
]
obtain a randomized
O(\log^{2}n\cdot\log k)
competitive ratio for the case where the host graph
G
is a tree, implying an
O(\log^{3}n\cdot\log k)
competitive ratio for general graphs.

In the online h.c. group Steiner tree problem, there is an additional requirement that number of hops in
T_{i}
between
r
to a member of
g_{i}
is at most
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}
, i.e.
\mathrm{hop}_{T_{i}}(r,g_{i})\leq{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}
.
Using their
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}
-Hop Repetition Tree Embedding,
[
HHZ21b
]
obtained hop-stretch
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}O(\log^{3}n)}
, and competitive ratio
O(\log^{5}n\cdot\log k)
.
That is, constructing a sequence of subgraphs
T_{1}\subseteq T_{2}\subseteq\dots
, such that for every
i
,
\mathrm{hop}_{T_{i}}(r,g_{i})\leq{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}O(\log^{3}n)\cdot h}
, and the (expected) weight of
T_{i}
is at most
O(\log^{5}n\cdot\log k)\cdot\mathrm{OPT}_{i}
, where
\mathrm{OPT}_{i}
is the cost of the optimal solution for the offline
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}
h.c. group Steiner tree problem for root
r
and groups
g_{1},\dots,g_{i}
.

Theorem 13
.

There is a poly-time randomized algorithm for the online
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}
-hop-constrained group Steiner tree problem on graphs with polynomial aspect ratio, that maintains a solution
\{T_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
which is

O(\log^{3}n\cdot\log k)
-cost competitive (i.e.
\mathbb{E}[w(T_{i})]\leq O(\log^{3}n\cdot\log k)\cdot\mathrm{OPT}_{i}
), and s.t.
\mathrm{hop}_{T_{i}}(r,g_{i})\leq{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}O(\log^{2}n)\cdot h}
for every
i
.
Alternatively, one can obtain a solution which is
\tilde{O}(\log^{3}n)\cdot\log k
-cost competitive, and s.t.
\mathrm{hop}_{T_{i}}(r,g_{i})\leq{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\tilde{O}(\log n)\cdot h}
for every
i
.

Note that ignoring the hop-constrains, our approximation factor is matching the state of the art
[
AAA+06
]
. It is also interesting to note that the only randomized component in our algorithm is the
[
AAA+06
]
black box solution for trees.

Proof of
Theorem

13
.

Consider an instance of the online
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}
-h.c. group Steiner
tree problem. Initially, we are given a graph
G=(V,E,w_{G})
, hop parameter
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}
, root
r\in V
, and a size
k
subset
\mathcal{K}\subseteq 2^{V}
of possible groups.
We apply
Corollary

1
with hop parameter
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h^{\prime}}={\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2h}
, stretch parameter
2
, and
r
as the special vertex. As a result we obtain path-tree one-to-many embedding
f
into a tree
\mathcal{T}
, with associated paths
\{P^{\mathcal{T}}_{e}\}_{e\in E(\mathcal{T})}
.

Next, we apply Alon
et al.
’s
[
AAA+06
]
algorithm for the online group Steiner tree problem on trees with competitive ratio
O(\log^{2}n\cdot\log k)
.
Here the input graph is
\mathcal{T}
, the root vertex is the only vertex
\tilde{r}
in
f(r)
, and the set of possible groups is
\tilde{\mathcal{K}}=\left\{f(K)\mid K\in\mathcal{K}\right\}
.
We will iteratively receive as an input the sets
g_{1},g_{2},\dots\in\mathcal{K}
, and feed Alon
et al.
’s
[
AAA+06
]
algorithm the sets
f(g_{1}),f(g_{2}),\dots\in\tilde{\mathcal{K}}
. As a result, we will obtain sub-trees
\tilde{T}_{1},\tilde{T}_{2},\dots
of
\mathcal{T}
.
For every
i
, we will return the solution
T_{i}=\cup_{e\in\tilde{T}_{i}}P^{\mathcal{T}}_{e}
.

We first argue that our solution is valid. As
\tilde{T}_{1}\subseteq\tilde{T}_{2}\subseteq\dots
, it holds that
T_{1}\subseteq T_{2}\subseteq\dots
.
Next,
\tilde{T}_{i}
contains a path
P_{i}=e_{1},\dots,e_{s}
from
\tilde{r}
to some vertex
v^{\prime}\in f(g_{i})
, in particular
v^{\prime}\in f(v)
for some
v\in g_{i}
. It follows that
T_{i}
will contain a path
P^{\mathcal{T}}_{\tilde{r},v^{\prime}}
between
r
to
v
. As the path-tree one-to-many embedding
f
has hop-bound
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}O(\log^{2}n)\cdot h^{\prime}}
, it follows that
\mathrm{hop}_{T_{i}}(r,g_{i})\leq{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}O(\log^{2}n)\cdot h}
as required.

Next, we bound the expected weight of the returned solution.
Let
H_{i}
be the optimal solution for the (offline)
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}
-h.c. group Steiner tree problem in the graph
G
with root
r
and groups
g_{1},\dots,g_{i}
, of cost
\mathrm{OPT}_{i}=w_{G}(H_{i})
.
Similarly to the proof of
Theorem

13
,
H_{i}
is a tree.
Note that for every
u\in H_{i}
,
\mathrm{hop}_{H_{i}}(r,u)\leq{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}
, as otherwise we can remove this vertex from
H_{i}
. As
H_{i}
is a tree, every pair of vertices
u,v\in H_{i}
, have a unique path between them with at most
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2h}
hops, in particular
d_{G}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(2h)}}(u,v)\leq d_{H_{i}}(u,v)
. We conclude that
H_{i}
is
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h^{\prime}}
-respecting.
Hence by
Corollary

1
,
T
contains a connected graph
\tilde{H}_{i}
, such that for every
u\in H_{i}
,
\tilde{H}_{i}
contains some vertex in
f(u)
, and
w_{T}(\tilde{H}_{i})=O(\log n)\cdot w_{G}(H_{i})=O(\log n)\cdot\mathrm{OPT}_{i}
.
Hence
\tilde{H}_{i}
is a valid solution to the group Steiner tree problem in
T
, and thus
\tilde{\mathrm{OPT}}_{i}=O(\log n)\cdot\mathrm{OPT}_{i}
, where
\tilde{\mathrm{OPT}}_{i}
denotes the cost of the optimal solution in
\tilde{H}_{i}
.
By
[
AAA+06
]
, it holds that

\mathbb{E}\left[w_{G}(T_{i})\right]\leq\mathbb{E}\left[w_{T}(\tilde{T}_{i})\right]\leq O(\log^{2}n\cdot\log k)\cdot\tilde{\mathrm{OPT}}_{i}=O(\log^{3}n\cdot\log k)\cdot\mathrm{OPT}_{i}\penalty\ .

The alternative tradeoff is obtained by using
Corollary

2
instead of
Corollary

1
.
∎

7.2.3
Hop-constrained group Steiner forest

In the group Steiner forest problem we are given
k
subset pairs
(S_{1},R_{1}),\dots,(S_{k},R_{k})\subseteq V
, where for every
i\in[k]
,
S_{i},R_{i}\subseteq V
. The goal is to construct a minimal weight forest
F
, such that for every
i
, there are vertices
s_{i}\in S_{i}
and
r_{i}\in R_{i}
, belonging to the same connected component of
F
.
The group Steiner forest problem is sometimes called “generalized
connectivity problem” as it generalizes both group Steiner tree, and Steiner forest problems.

The group Steiner forest problem was introduced by Alon
et al.
[
AAA+06
]
.
The state of the art is by Chekuri
et al.
[
CEGS11
]
who obtained an
O(\log^{2}n\log^{2}k)
approximation factor.

In the hop-constrained version of the problem (introduced by
[
HHZ21b
]
), we are given in addition a hop-bound
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}
, and the requirement is to find a minimum weight subgraph
H
, such that for every
i
,
\mathrm{hop}_{H}(S_{i},R_{i})\leq{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}
.
Denote by
\mathrm{OPT}
the weight of the optimal solution.

[
HHZ21b
]
obtained a bicriteria approximation by constructing a subgraph
H
of weight
O(\log^{6}n\cdot\log k)\cdot\mathrm{OPT}
, such that for every
i
,
\mathrm{hop}_{H}(r,g_{i})\leq{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}O(\log^{3}n)\cdot h}
.
We obtain the following:
See
7

Proof.

We apply
Theorem

5
(with hop parameter
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}
, and stretch parameter
2
) to obtain path-tree one-to-many embedding
f
into a tree
T
, with associated paths
\{P^{T}_{e}\}_{e\in E(T)}
. For every
i
, let
\tilde{S}_{i}=f(S_{i})
, and
\tilde{R}_{i}=f(R_{i})
.
Naor, Panigrahi, and Singh
[
NPS11
]
constructed an
O(d\cdot\log^{2}n\cdot\log k)
approximation algorithm for the group Steiner forest problem in trees of depth
d
.
10
10

10

The paper
[
NPS11
]
deals only with the online version of the group Steiner forest problem. From private communication with Hershkowitz
[
Her22
]
, the
O(d\cdot\log^{2}n\cdot\log k)
approximation factor follows from
[
NPS11
]
implicitly.

Following the discussion in the proof of
Lemma

15
the tree returned by
Corollary

1
(and therefore also
Theorem

5
) has depth
O(\log n)
.
We apply
[
NPS11
]
algorithm on the tree
T
with the group pairs
(\tilde{S}_{1},\tilde{T}_{1}),\dots,(\tilde{S}_{k},\tilde{T}_{k})
. Suppose that
[
NPS11
]
return the solution
\tilde{H}\subseteq T
. Our solution to the original problem will be
H=\bigcup_{e\in\tilde{H}}P^{T}_{e}
.

For every
i
,
\tilde{H}
contains a path
P_{i}=e_{1},\dots,e_{s}
from a vertex
s^{\prime}\in\tilde{S}_{i}=f(S_{i})
to a vertex
r^{\prime}\in\tilde{R}_{i}=f(R_{i})
.
It follows that the solution
H
we will return, will contain a path
P^{T}_{s^{\prime},r^{\prime}}
between
f^{-1}(s^{\prime})\in S_{i}
to
f^{-1}(r^{\prime})\in R_{i}
. As the path-tree one-to-many embedding
f
has hop-bound
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}O(\log^{3}n)\cdot h}
, it follows that
\mathrm{hop}_{H}(S_{i},R_{i})\leq{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}O(\log^{3}n)\cdot h}
as required.
We conclude that
H
is a valid solution to the problem.

Next we bound the weight of
H
. Let
F
be an optimal solution in
G
of weight
\mathrm{OPT}
.
By
Theorem

5
,
T
contains a subgraph
\tilde{F}
, such that for every
u,v\in F
with
d_{F}(u,v)\leq{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}
,
\tilde{F}
contains two vertices from
f(u)
and
f(v)
in the same connected component, and
w_{T}(\tilde{F})=O(\log n)\cdot w_{G}(F)=O(\log n)\cdot\mathrm{OPT}
.
Note that
\tilde{F}
is a valid solution to the h.c. group Steiner forest problem in
T
. Hence by
[
NPS11
]
,

w_{G}(H)\leq w_{T}(\tilde{H})\leq O(\log^{3}n\cdot\log k)\cdot w_{T}(\tilde{F})=O(\log^{4}n\cdot\log k)\cdot\mathrm{OPT}\penalty\ .

as required.
The alternative tradeoff is obtained by using
Theorem

11
instead of
Theorem

5
.
∎

7.2.4
Online hop-constrained group Steiner forest

The online group Steiner tree forest is an online version of the group Steiner forest problem. Here we are given a graph
G=(V,E,w)
, and subset pairs
(S_{1},R_{1}),\dots,(S_{i},R_{i}),\dots
(where
S_{i},R_{i}\subseteq V
) arrive one at a time. The goal is to maintain forests
F_{1},F_{2},\dots
such that for every
i
, there are vertices
s_{i}\in S_{i}
and
r_{i}\in R_{i}
, belonging to the same connected component of
F_{i}
.
An algorithm has
competitive ratio

t
if for every sequence of subset pairs
(S_{1},R_{1}),\dots,(S_{i},R_{i})
, the forest
F_{i}
has weight at most
t
times larger than the optimal solution for the offline group Steiner forest problem with input subset pairs
(S_{1},R_{1}),\dots,(S_{i},R_{i})
.
For randomized algorithms, the competitive ratio is the worst case expected ratio between the weights of the constructed forest to that of the optimal solution.
It follows from Alon
et al.
[
AAA+06
]
that in full generality no sub-polynomial approximation is possible.
Therefore, we will assume that there is a set of subset pairs
\mathcal{K}\subseteq 2^{V}\times 2^{V}
of size
k
, and all the subset pairs
(S_{i},R_{i})
are chosen from
\mathcal{K}
.
For this version, Naor
et al.
[
NPS11
]
obtain an
O(\log^{5}n\cdot\log k)
competitive ratio for the Online group Steiner forest (answering an open question of
[
CEGS11
]
).

In the online h.c. group Steiner tree problem (introduced by
[
HHZ21b
]
), there is an additional requirement that number of hops in
F_{i}
between a member of
S_{i}
to a member of
R_{i}
is at most
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}
, i.e.
\mathrm{hop}_{F_{i}}(S_{i},R_{i})\leq{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}
.
Using their
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}
-Hop Repetition Tree Embedding,
[
HHZ21b
]
obtained hop-stretch
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}O(\log^{3}n)}
, and competitive ratio
O(\log^{7}n\cdot\log k)
.
That is, constructing a sequence of subgraphs
F_{1}\subseteq F_{2}\subseteq\dots
, such that for every
i
,
\mathrm{hop}_{F_{i}}(S_{i},R_{i})\leq{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}O(\log^{3}n)\cdot h}
, and the (expected) weight of
F_{i}
is at most
O(\log^{7}n\cdot\log k)\cdot\mathrm{OPT}_{i}
, where
\mathrm{OPT}_{i}
is the cost of the optimal solution for the offline
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}
h.c. group Steiner forest problem for subset pairs
(S_{1},R_{1}),\dots,(S_{i},R_{i})
.
Using our
Theorems

5
and
11
we obtain a significant improvement:

Theorem 14
.

There is a poly-time randomized algorithm for the online
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}
-hop-constrained group Steiner forest problem on graphs with polynomial aspect ratio, that maintains a solution
\{F_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
which is

O(\log^{5}n\cdot\log k)
-cost competitive (i.e.
\mathbb{E}[w(F_{i})]\leq O(\log^{5}n\cdot\log k)\cdot\mathrm{OPT}_{i}
), and s.t.
\mathrm{hop}_{F_{i}}(S_{i},R_{i})\leq{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}O(\log^{3}n)\cdot h}
for every
i
.
Alternatively, one can obtain a solution which is
\tilde{O}(\log^{5}n)\cdot\log k
-cost competitive, and s.t.
\mathrm{hop}_{F_{i}}(S_{i},R_{i})\leq{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\tilde{O}(\log^{2}n)\cdot h}
for every
i
.

Note that ignoring the hop-constrains, our competitive ratio matching the state of the art
[
NPS11
]
.

Proof of
Theorem

14
.

Consider an instance of the online
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}
-h.c. group Steiner forest problem. Initially, we are given a graph
G=(V,E,w_{G})
, hop parameter
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}
, and a set of possible subset pairs
\mathcal{K}\subseteq 2^{V}\times 2^{V}
of size
k
.
We apply
Theorem

5
to obtain path-tree one-to-many embedding
f
into a tree
T
, with associated paths
\{P^{T}_{e}\}_{e\in E(T)}
.
Naor, Panigrahi, and Singh
[
NPS11
]
constructed an online algorithm for the group Steiner forest problems on trees of depth
d
with competitive ratio
O(d\cdot\log^{3}n\cdot\log k)
.
Following the discussion in the proof of
Lemma

15
the tree returned by
Corollary

1
(and therefore also
Theorem

5
) has depth
O(\log n)
.

We apply
[
NPS11
]
online algorithm on the tree
T
with the set of possible subset pairs
\tilde{\mathcal{K}}=\{(f(S_{i}),f(R_{i}))\mid(S_{i},R_{i})\in\mathcal{K}\}
.
We will iteratively receive as an input subset pairs
(S_{1},R_{1}),(S_{2},R_{2}),\dots\in\mathcal{K}
, and feed Naor
et al.
[
NPS11
]
algorithm the subset pairs
(f(S_{1}),f(R_{1})),(f(S_{2}),f(R_{2})),\dots\in\tilde{\mathcal{K}}
. As a result, we will obtain forests
\tilde{F}_{1},\tilde{F}_{2},\dots
of
T
.
For every
i
, we will return the solution
\mathcal{F}_{i}=\cup_{e\in\tilde{F}_{i}}P^{T}_{e}
.

For every
i
,
\tilde{F}_{i}
contains a path
P_{i}=e_{1},\dots,e_{s}
from a vertex
s^{\prime}\in f(S_{i})
to a vertex
r^{\prime}\in f(R_{i})
.
It follows that the solution we will return
\mathcal{F}_{i}
, will contain a path
P^{T}_{s^{\prime},r^{\prime}}
between
f^{-1}(s^{\prime})\in S_{i}
to
f^{-1}(r^{\prime})\in R_{i}
. As the path-tree one-to-many embedding
f
has hop-bound
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}O(\log^{3}n)\cdot h}
, it follows that
\mathrm{hop}_{\mathcal{F}_{i}}(S_{i},R_{i})\leq{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}O(\log^{3}n)\cdot h}
as required.
We conclude that
\mathcal{F}_{i}
is a valid solution to the problem.

Next we bound the weight of
\mathcal{F}_{i}
. Let
H_{i}
be an optimal solution in
G
of weight
\mathrm{OPT}
on input
(S_{1},R_{1}),\dots,(S_{i},R_{i})
.
By
Theorem

5
,
T
contains a subgraph
\tilde{H}_{i}
, such that for every
u,v\in H_{i}
with
d_{H_{i}}(u,v)\leq{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}
,
\tilde{H}_{i}
contains two vertices from
f(u)
and
f(v)
in the same connected component, and
w_{T}(\tilde{H}_{i})=O(\log n)\cdot w_{G}(H_{i})=O(\log n)\cdot\mathrm{OPT}
.
Note that
\tilde{H}_{i}
is a valid solution to the group Steiner forest problem in
T
. Hence by
[
NPS11
]
,

\mathbb{E}\left[w_{G}(\mathcal{F}_{i})\right]\leq\mathbb{E}\left[w_{T}(\tilde{F}_{i})\right]\leq O(\log^{4}n\cdot\log k)\cdot w_{T}(\tilde{H}_{i})=O(\log^{5}n\cdot\log k)\cdot\mathrm{OPT}\penalty\ .

as required.
The alternative tradeoff is obtained by using
Theorem

11
instead of
Theorem

5
.
∎

8
Hop-constrained metric data structures

In this section we construct hop-constrained metric data structures.
Mendel and Naor
[
MN07
]
(and later Abraham
et al.
[
ACE+20
]
) used Ramsey trees in order to construct metric data structures. This is done by simply constructing Ramsey trees until every vertex belongs to the set
M
in some tree. Here we begin by following the exact same approach using
Lemma

4
to obtain
Lemmas

17
and
18
below.

The distortion factor in
Lemmas

17
and
18
is large, and we can improve it significantly. In our actual metric data structures we will use
Lemmas

17
and
18
as a subroutine to obtain an initial coarse estimation of the hop-constrained distance. Given such estimation
d^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(h)}}_{G}(u,v)\approx 2^{i}
, we can construct an auxilery graph
G_{i}
where the shortest path distance (not hop-constrained) is an accurate estimation of
d^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(h)}}_{G}(u,v)
. We then use state of the art metric data structures on top of
G_{i}
in a black box manner.

We begin by a construction of an asymmetric version of a distance labeling, where each vertex has a short, and long labels.

Lemma 17
.

Given a weighted graph
G=(V,E,w)
on
n
vertices with polynomial aspect ratio, and parameters
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
\epsilon\in(0,1)
,
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}\in\mathbb{N}
, there is an efficient construction of a distance labelings that assigns each node
v
a label
\ell(v)
consisting of two parts
\ell^{S}(v),\ell^{L}(v)
such that:

\ell^{S}(v)
has size
O(1)
,
\ell^{L}(v)
has size
O(n^{\frac{1}{k}}\cdot k)
, and there is an algorithm
\mathcal{A}
that for every pair of vertices
u,v
, given
\ell^{L}(v),\ell^{S}(u)
returns a value
\mathcal{A}(\ell^{L}(v),\ell^{S}(u))
such that
d^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(O(k\cdot\log\log n)\cdot h)}}_{G}(u,v)\leq\mathcal{A}(\ell^{L}(v),\ell^{S}(u))\leq O(k\cdot\log\log n)\cdot d^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(h)}}_{G}(u,v)
.

Proof.

Set
\mathcal{M}_{0}=V
.
For
i\geq 1
, apply
Lemma

4

11
11

11

One might wonder why we use here
Lemma

4
, and whether other interesting tradeoff can be obtained if we instead will use
Lemma

1
. The answer is that
Lemma

17
here is not our end goal, but will only be used by
Theorems

8
,
9
and
10
to get an initial rough estimate on the h.c. distance. In particular, there is no significant difference between distortion
O(k)
and
O(k\cdot\log\log n)
. However, the difference between hop-stretch
O(k\cdot\log\log n)
and
O(k\cdot\log n)
(which we will get by using
Lemma

1
) is very significant. We are therefore using here only
Lemma

4
.
with parameters
k,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h},\mathcal{M}_{i-1}
, and uniform measure
\mu
to construct a Ramsey type embedding into ultrametric
U_{i}
with Ramsey hop distortion
(O(k\cdot\log\log n),M_{i},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}O(k\cdot\log\log n)},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h})
. Set
\mathcal{M}_{i}=\mathcal{M}_{i-1}\backslash M_{i}
.
This process halts once
\mathcal{M}_{i}=\emptyset
.

To analyze the number of steps until the process halts, denote by
i_{1}
the first time
|\mathcal{M}_{i_{1}}|\leq\frac{1}{2}n
.
As long as
i<i_{1}
,
|M_{i}|=\mu(M_{i})\geq\mu(\mathcal{M}_{i-1})^{1-\frac{1}{k}}\geq(\frac{n}{2})^{1-\frac{1}{k}}
.
Thus for each such step we remove from
\mathcal{M}_{i}
at least
(\frac{n}{2})^{1-\frac{1}{k}}
, until we reach
i_{1}
.
It follows that
i_{1}\leq\frac{n/2}{(\frac{n}{2})^{1-\frac{1}{k}}}=(\frac{n}{2})^{\frac{1}{k}}
.
Similarly, let
i_{2}
be the first index such that
|\mathcal{M}_{i_{1}+i_{2}}|\leq\frac{n}{4}
. The same calculation will provide us with
i_{2}\leq(\frac{n}{4})^{\frac{1}{k}}
.
Following this line of thought, the process must halt after at most

\sum_{i=1}^{\log n}(\frac{n}{2^{i}})^{\frac{1}{k}}\leq n^{\frac{1}{k}}\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}2^{-\frac{i}{k}}=n^{\frac{1}{k}}\cdot\frac{2^{-\frac{1}{k}}}{1-2^{-\frac{1}{k}}}=O(n^{\frac{1}{k}}\cdot k)

rounds.
For every vertex
v\in V
, denote by
\mbox{\bf home}(v)
some index
i
such that
v\in M_{i}
.

A basic ingredient for our data structure will be distance labeling for trees.
Exact distance labeling on an
n
-vertex tree requires
\Theta(\log n)
words
[
AGHP16
]
. In order to shave a
\log n
factor, we will use approximated labeling scheme.
Freedman
et al.
[
FGNW17
]
(improving upon
[
AGHP16
,
GKK+01
]
) showed that for any
n
-vertex unweighted tree, and
\delta\in(0,1)
, one can construct an
(1+\delta)
-labeling scheme with labels of size
O(\log\frac{1}{\delta})
words.

Theorem 15

(
[
FGNW17
]
)
.

For any
n
-vertex unweighted tree
T=(V,E)
and parameter
\delta\in(0,1)
, there is a distance labeling scheme with stretch
1+\delta
, label size
O(\log\frac{1}{\delta})
, and
O(1)
query time.

The result of
[
FGNW17
]
cannot be improved even when restricted to the case of (polynomially weighted) ultrametrics
12
12

12

The lower bound is based on
[
AGHP16
]

(h,M)
-trees, and uses only the labels on leaf vertices. One can observe that the metric induces by leaf vertices in
(h,M)
-trees is in fact an ultrametric.
.
We will use
Theorem

15
with
\delta=\frac{1}{2}
. For this case the theorem can be extended to weighted trees with polynomial aspect ratio (by subdividing edges).

For every ultrametric
U_{i}
, we will construct a distance labeling using
Theorem

15
with parameter
\delta=\frac{1}{2}
, where the label of
v
is
\ell_{i}(v)
.
For every vertex
v\in V
,
\ell^{S}(v)
will consist of
\ell_{\mbox{\bf home}(v)}(v)
and the index
\mbox{\bf home}(v)
, while
\ell_{L}(v)
will be the concatenation of
\ell_{i}(v)
for all the constructed ultrametrics. The size guarantees clearly hold, while given
\ell^{L}(v),\ell^{S}(u)
, we can in
O(1)
time report the approximated distance between
u
to
v
in
U_{\mbox{\bf home}(u)}
, which satisfies:

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}(\ell_{\mbox{\bf home}(u)}(v),\ell_{\mbox{\bf home}(u)}(u))

\displaystyle\leq(1+\delta)\cdot d_{U_{\mbox{\bf home}(u)}}(u,v)=O(k\cdot\log\log n)\cdot d_{G}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(h)}}(u,v)\penalty\ .

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}(\ell_{\mbox{\bf home}(u)}(v),\ell_{\mbox{\bf home}(u)}(u))

\displaystyle\geq d_{U_{\mbox{\bf home}(u)}}(u,v)\geq d_{G}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(O(k\cdot\log\log n)\cdot h)}}(u,v)\penalty\ .

∎

Next we construct a distance oracle.

Lemma 18
.

Given a weighted graph
G=(V,E,w)
on
n
vertices with polynomial aspect ratio, and parameters
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
\epsilon\in(0,1)
,
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}\in\mathbb{N}
, there is an efficient construction of a distance oracle
\mathsf{DO}
of size
O(n^{1+\frac{1}{k}})
, which given a query
(u,v)
, in
O(1)
times returns a value such that
d^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(O(k\cdot\log\log n)\cdot h)}}_{G}(u,v)\leq\mathsf{DO}(u,v)\leq O(k\cdot\log\log n)\cdot d^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(h)}}_{G}(u,v)
.

Proof.

We will slightly modify the construction in
Lemma

17
.
For
i\geq 1
, apply
Theorem

2

13
13

13

Similarly to
footnote

11
, no real advantage can be obtained here by replacing
Theorem

2
with
Theorem

1
.
to construct an ultrametric
U_{i}
with set
M_{i}
, such that for every
v\in V
,
\Pr[v\in M_{i}]\geq\Omega(n^{-\frac{1}{k}})
, and such that for every
v\in M_{i}
,
u\in V
it holds that
d_{G}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(O(k\cdot\log\log n)\cdot h)}}(u,v)\leq d_{U_{i}}(u,v)\leq O(k\cdot\log\log n)\cdot d_{G}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(h)}}(u,v)
.

For each vertex
v\in V
, let
\mbox{\bf home}(v)
be the minimal index
i
such that
v\in M_{i}
. Note that
\mbox{\bf home}(v)
is distributed according to geometric distribution with parameter
\Omega(n^{-\frac{1}{k}})
. In particular

\mathbb{E}[\mbox{\bf home}(v)]=O(n^{\frac{1}{k}})
.

For each index
i
, we will apply
Theorem

15
(with
\delta=\frac{1}{2}
) on the ultrametric
U_{i}
, to obtain distance labeling
\{\ell_{i}(v)\}_{v\in V}
.
In our distance oracle, for every vertex
v
we will store the index
\mbox{\bf home}(v)
and the labels of
v
in the first
\mbox{\bf home}(v)
ultrametrics:

\ell_{1}(v),\dots,\ell_{\mbox{\bf home}(v)}(v)
. The expected size of the distance oracle is

\mathbb{E}[|\mathsf{DO}|]=\sum_{v\in V}\mathbb{E}[\mbox{\bf home}(v)]\cdot O(1)=O(n^{1+\frac{1}{k}})\penalty\ .

After several attempts (using Markov inequality), we can construct distance oracle of
O(n^{1+\frac{1}{k}})
size.

Given a query
u,v
, let
i=\min\{\mbox{\bf home}(v),\mbox{\bf home}(u)\}
, we will use
[
FGNW17
]
algorithm on
\ell_{i}(v),\ell_{i}(u)
.
As a result, we will obtain an estimation

d^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(O(k\cdot\log\log n)\cdot h)}}_{G}(u,v)\leq d_{U_{i}}(u,v)\leq\mathcal{A}(u,v)\leq(1+\delta)\cdot d_{U_{i}}(u,v)=O(k\cdot\log\log n)\cdot d^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(h)}}_{G}(u,v)\penalty\ .

Finally note that both finding
\mbox{\bf home}(v)
, and computing the estimation using
[
FGNW17
]
takes
O(1)
time. ∎

While
Lemmas

17
and
18
already provide h.c. labeling scheme and distance oracles, we can significantly improve the stretch guarantee (even up to
1+\epsilon
).
Next we will construct auxiliary graphs
\{G_{i}\}_{i\geq 0}
. Originally these graphs were constructed by
[
HHZ21b
]
, and used to construct padded decompositions. Later, we will construct our metric data structures upon these graphs.
Fix
\beta=O(k\cdot\log\log n)
such
that the distance estimation
\mathsf{DO}(u,v)
in
Lemma

18
(or
\mathcal{A}(\ell(v),\ell(u))
in
Lemma

17
) satisfies
d_{G}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(\beta\cdot h)}}(u,v)\leq\mathsf{DO}(u,v)\leq\beta\cdot d_{G}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(h)}}(u,v)
.
Let
G_{i}=(V,E,w_{i})
be the graph where the weight of every edge was increased by an additive factor of
\omega_{i}=\frac{\epsilon}{\beta\cdot h}\cdot 2^{i}
.
That is, for every edge
e
,
w_{i}(e)=w(e)+\omega_{i}
. Suppose,
that
2^{i}\leq\mathsf{DO}(u,v)<2^{i+1}
. We have that

\displaystyle d_{G}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(\beta\cdot h)}}(u,v)

\displaystyle\leq\mathsf{DO}(u,v)\leq 2^{i+1}

\displaystyle 2^{i}

\displaystyle\leq\mathsf{DO}(u,v)\leq\beta\cdot d_{G}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(h)}}(u,v)\penalty\ .

Consider
G_{i}
, by looking on the shortest
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}
-hop
u
-
v
path in
G
we have that

d_{G_{i}}(u,v)\leq d_{G}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(h)}}(u,v)+h\cdot\omega_{i}\leq d_{G}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(h)}}(u,v)+\frac{\epsilon}{\beta}\cdot 2^{i}=(1+\epsilon)\cdot d_{G}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(h)}}(u,v)\penalty\ .

(9)

From the other hand

\displaystyle d_{G_{i}}(u,v)

\displaystyle\geq\min\left\{d_{G}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(\frac{2\beta\cdot h}{\epsilon})}}(u,v),\frac{2\beta\cdot h}{\epsilon}\cdot\omega_{i}\right\}=\min\left\{d_{G}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(\frac{2\beta\cdot h}{\epsilon})}}(u,v),2^{i+1}\right\}\overset{(*)}{=}d_{G}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(O(\frac{k\cdot\log\log n}{\epsilon})\cdot h)}}(u,v)\penalty\ .

(10)

Here the first inequality holds as if the shortest
u
-
v
path in
G_{i}
has less than
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\frac{2\beta\cdot h}{\epsilon}}
-hops than
d_{G_{i}}(u,v)\geq d_{G}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(\frac{2\beta\cdot h}{\epsilon})}}(u,v)
, while otherwise
d_{G_{i}}(u,v)\geq\frac{2\beta\cdot h}{\epsilon}\cdot\omega_{i}
.
The equality
(*)
follows as
d_{G}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(\frac{2\beta\cdot h}{\epsilon})}}(u,v)\leq d_{G}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(\beta\cdot h)}}(u,v)\leq 2^{i+1}
.
Furthermore, consider a path
P
in
G_{i}
of weight at most
O(k)\cdot d_{G_{i}}(u,v)\leq O(k)\cdot d_{G}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(h)}}(u,v)\leq O(k)\cdot 2^{i}
.
Then

\mathrm{hop}(P)\leq\frac{w_{i}(P)}{\omega_{i}}={\color[rgb]{1,0,0}O(\frac{k\cdot\beta}{\epsilon})\cdot h}={\color[rgb]{1,0,0}O(\frac{k^{2}\cdot\log\log n}{\epsilon})\cdot h}\penalty\ .

(11)

8.1
Distance oracles.

This section is devoted to proving
Theorem

8
, we restate it for convenience:
See
8

We will use Chechik’s distance oracle:

Theorem 16

(
[
Che15
]
)
.

Given a weighted graph
G=(V,E,w)
on
n
vertices and parameter
k\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists a distance oracle
\mathsf{DO}
of size
O(n^{1+\frac{1}{k}})
, which given a query
(u,v)
, in
O(1)
time returns a value such that
d_{G}(u,v)\leq\mathsf{DO}(u,v)\leq(2k-1)\cdot d_{G}(u,v)
.

Let
D
be the maximal distance of a pair with at most
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}
hops, among all pairs with finite
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}
-hop distance, that is
D=\max_{u,v\in V}\{d_{G}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(h)}}(u,v)\mid d_{G}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(h)}}(u,v)<\infty\}
. Suppose w.l.o.g. that the minimal distance is
1
.
For every
i\in\left[0,\log\left(O(D\cdot k\cdot\log\log n)\right)\right]
,
let
G_{i}=(V,E,w_{i})
be the graph defined above (w.r.t.
\omega_{i}
).
We use
Theorem

16
to construct distance oracle
\mathsf{DO}_{i}
for
G_{i}
.
Our distance oracle
\mathsf{DO}
will consist of the distance oracle
\widetilde{\mathsf{DO}}
constructed in
Lemma

18
, and
\mathsf{DO}_{i}
for
i\in\left[0,\log\left(O(D\cdot k\cdot\log\log n)\right)\right]
. Clearly, the size of
\mathsf{DO}
is bounded by
O(n^{1+\frac{1}{k}}\cdot\log n)
.

Given a query
(u,v)
, we first query
\widetilde{\mathsf{DO}}
, to obtain estimation such that

1\leq d^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(O(k\cdot\log\log n)\cdot h)}}_{G}(u,v)\leq\widetilde{\mathsf{DO}}(u,v)\leq O(k\cdot\log\log n)\cdot d^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(h)}}_{G}(u,v)
.
If
\widetilde{\mathsf{DO}}(u,v)>\Omega(k\cdot\log\log n)\cdot D
(with the same constant as in the upper bound in
Lemma

18
), then

d^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(h)}}_{G}(u,v)>D
and we will just return
\infty
. Otherwise,
\widetilde{\mathsf{DO}}(u,v)\leq\Omega(k\cdot\log\log n)\cdot D
.
Let
i
be the unique index such that

2^{i}\leq\widetilde{\mathsf{DO}}(u,v)<2^{i+1}
. We will return
\mathsf{DO}_{i}(u,v)
.
As we invoked two distance oracles, each with
O(1)
query time, our query time is
O(1)
as well.

Finally, for the approximation factor, by equations (
9
) and (
10
) it holds that

d_{G}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(O(\frac{k\cdot\log\log n}{\epsilon})\cdot h)}}(u,v)\leq d_{G_{i}}(u,v)\leq(1+\epsilon)\cdot d_{G}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(h)}}(u,v)\penalty\ .

Thus by
Theorem

16
, it will hold that

d_{G}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(O(\frac{k\cdot\log\log n}{\epsilon})\cdot h)}}(u,v)\leq\mathsf{DO}_{i}(u,v)\leq(2k-1)(1+\epsilon)\cdot d_{G}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(h)}}(u,v)\penalty\ .

8.2
Distance labeling.

This section is devoted to proving
Theorem

9
, we restate it for convenience:
See
9

We will use Thorup Zwick distance labeling:

Theorem 17

(
[
TZ05
]
)
.

Given a weighted graph
G=(V,E,w)
on
n
vertices, and parameter
k\in\mathbb{N}
, there is an efficient construction of a distance labeling that assigns each node
v
a label
\ell(v)
of size
O(n^{\frac{1}{k}}\cdot\log n)
, and such that there is an algorithm
\mathcal{A}
that on input
\ell(u),\ell(v)
, in
O(k)
time returns a value such that
d_{G}(u,v)\leq\mathcal{A}(\ell(v),\ell(u))\leq(2k-1)\cdot d_{G}(u,v)
.

We will follow the lines of the construction in
Section

8.1
.
Recall that
D=\max_{u,v\in V}\{d_{G}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(h)}}(u,v)\mid d_{G}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(h)}}(u,v)<\infty\}
, and the minimum distance is
1
.
For every
i\in\left[0,\log\left(O(D\cdot k\cdot\log\log n)\right)\right]
,
let
G_{i}=(V,E,w_{i})
be the graph defined above (w.r.t.
\omega_{i}
).
We use
Theorem

17
to construct distance labeling
\ell_{i}
for
G_{i}
.
The label
\ell(v)
of a vertex
v
will consist of the label
\tilde{\ell}(v)
constructed in
Lemma

17
(with associated algorithm
\tilde{\mathcal{A}}
), and
\ell_{i}(v)
for
i\in\left[0,\log\left(O(D\cdot k\cdot\log\log n)\right)\right]
(with associated algorithm
\mathcal{A}_{i}
). Clearly, the size of
\ell(v)
is bounded by
O(n^{1+\frac{1}{k}}\cdot\log^{2}n)
.

Given
\ell(v),\ell(u)
, we first compute
\tilde{\mathcal{A}}(\tilde{\ell}(v),\tilde{\ell}(u))
, to obtain estimation such that

1\leq d^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(O(k\cdot\log\log n)\cdot h)}}_{G}(u,v)\leq\tilde{\mathcal{A}}(\tilde{\ell}(v),\tilde{\ell}(u))\leq O(k\cdot\log\log n)\cdot d^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(h)}}_{G}(u,v)
.
If
\tilde{\mathcal{A}}(\tilde{\ell}(v),\tilde{\ell}(u))>\Omega(k\cdot\log\log n)\cdot D
, then
d^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(h)}}_{G}(u,v)>D
and we simply return
\infty
.
Otherwise,
d^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(h)}}_{G}(u,v)\leq O(k\cdot\log\log n)\cdot D
.
Let
i
be the unique index such that

2^{i}\leq\tilde{\mathcal{A}}(\tilde{\ell}(v),\tilde{\ell}(u))<2^{i+1}
. We will return
\mathcal{A}_{i}\left(\ell_{i}(v),\ell_{i}(u)\right)
.
We invoked two labeling algorithms, the first with
O(1)
query time, while the second had query time
O(k)
. Hence our query time is
O(k)
.
Finally, for the approximation factor, by equations (
9
), (
10
), and
Theorem

17
it holds that

d_{G}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(O(\frac{k\cdot\log\log n}{\epsilon})\cdot h)}}(u,v)\leq\mathcal{A}(\ell(v),\ell(u))\leq(2k-1)(1+\epsilon)\cdot d_{G}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(h)}}(u,v)\penalty\ .

Remark 2
.

If instead of using
[
TZ05
]
distance labeling, one would use a Ramsey tree based one (such as in
Lemma

17
), it is possible to obtain constant query time
O(1)
, while the stretch will increase from
(2k-1)(1+\epsilon)
to
2ek\cdot(1+\epsilon)
(using the best known Ramsey trees
[
NT12
]
).

8.3
Compact routing scheme.

This section is devoted to proving
Theorem

10
, we restate it for convenience:
See
10

We will use Chechik’s compact routing scheme:

Theorem 18

(
[
Che13
]
)
.

For every weighted graph
G=(V,E,w)
on
n
vertices and parameter
k\in\mathbb{N}
, there is an efficient construction of a compact routing scheme that assigns each node a table of size
O(k\cdot n^{\frac{1}{k}})
, label of size
O(k\cdot\log n)
, and such that the routing a packet from
u
to
v
, will be done using a path
P
such that
w(P)\leq 3.68k\cdot d_{G}(u,v)
.

We will follow the lines of the construction in
Section

8.2
.
Recall that
D=\max_{u,v\in V}\{d_{G}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(h)}}(u,v)\mid d_{G}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(h)}}(u,v)<\infty\}
, and the minimal distance is
1
.
For every
i\in\left[0,\log\left(O(D\cdot k\cdot\log\log n)\right)\right]
,
let
G_{i}=(V,E,w_{i})
be the graph defined above (w.r.t.
\omega_{i}
).
We use
Theorem

18
to construct compact routing scheme for each
G_{i}
, where the label and table of
v
denoted by
L_{i}(v),T_{i}(v)
, and have sizes
O(k\cdot\log n)
and
O(k\cdot n^{\frac{1}{k}})
, respectively.
In addition, we construct asymmetric distance labeling scheme using
Lemma

17
, where each vertex
v
get two labels
\ell^{S}(v),\ell^{L}(v)
of sizes
O(1),O(n^{\frac{1}{k}}\cdot k)
respectively.

For the routing scheme, the label
L(v)
of a vertex
v
will consist of
\ell^{S}(v)
and
L_{i}(v)
for every
i\in\left[0,\log\left(O(D\cdot k\cdot\log\log n)\right)\right]
. Note that
L(v)
has size
O(k\cdot\log^{2}n)
.
The table
T(v)
of
v
will consist of
\ell^{L}(v)
and
T_{i}(v)
for every
i\in\left[0,\log\left(O(D\cdot k\cdot\log\log n)\right)\right]
. Thus
T(v)
has size
O(n^{\frac{1}{k}}\cdot k\cdot\log n)
.

Suppose that we wish to sent a packet from
v
to
u
. First, using the distance labeling
\ell^{L}(v)
and
\ell^{S}(u)
, we compute an estimate
\mathcal{A}(\ell^{L}(v),\ell^{S}(u))
such that

d^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(O(k\cdot\log\log n)\cdot h)}}_{G}(u,v)\leq\mathcal{A}(\ell^{L}(v),\ell^{S}(u))\leq O(k\cdot\log\log n)\cdot d^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(h)}}_{G}(u,v)
.
If
\mathcal{A}(\ell^{L}(v),\ell^{S}(u))>\Omega(k\cdot\log\log n)\cdot D
, then
d^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(h)}}_{G}(u,v)>D
(implying
\mathrm{hop}_{G}(u,v)>{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h}
) and we can do nothing (as we are not required to deliver the package). Otherwise,
\mathcal{A}(\ell^{L}(v),\ell^{S}(u))\leq\Omega(k\cdot\log\log n)\cdot D
.
Let
i
be the unique index such that

2^{i}\leq\mathcal{A}(\ell^{L}(v),\ell^{S}(u))<2^{i+1}
. We will sent the packet using the routing scheme constructed for
G_{i}
(the index
i
will be attached to the header of each message).
Then the packet will reach
u
using a path
P
such that
w(P)<w_{i}(P)\leq 3.68k\cdot d_{G_{i}}(u,v)\overset{(\ref{eq:Gi_UB})}{\leq}3.68k\cdot(1+\epsilon)\cdot d^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(h)}}_{G}(u,v)
, while the number of hops in
P
is bounded by
\mathrm{hop}(P)\overset{(\ref{eq:Gi_hops})}{\leq}O(\frac{k^{2}\cdot\log\log n}{\epsilon})\cdot h
.
